- Review your core beliefs every day to keep them active in your subconscious. Write down which beliefs you still struggle with and act on the ones that feel uncomfortable. Repeated review helps the new beliefs replace old automatic thoughts. [L1513.025]
- A new belief has settled into the subconscious when reviewing it produces an immediate felt sense of recognition, like a sudden "I get it" moment, rather than only intellectual agreement. After reviewing each belief, check for that felt response, and if it does not appear, reflect on that belief again before moving on. [L1513.030]

### Testing limiting beliefs

- Rewrite a limiting story that blocks a task:
  1. Write down, in one sentence, the story you tell yourself when a task becomes hard. [L0378.120]
  2. Identify the expectation behind it, such as what should feel comfortable or what should already be true. [L0378.120]
  3. Compare that expectation with what is actually happening and note the gap. [L0378.120]
  4. Write a new sentence you can accept without pretending the discomfort is gone. [L0378.120]
  5. Repeat the new sentence before the next hard task and track whether the old story still drives your decisions. [L0378.120]
- Testing a limiting belief with four questions:
  1. Name the belief behind the behavior, for example that you cannot do a certain task, or that you deserve less. [L1206.011]
  2. Ask whether it rests on an actual fact or only on an assumption or a false conclusion. [L1206.011]
  3. Ask whether there is any rational reason to hold it. [L1206.011]
  4. Ask whether you could be wrong, and whether you would reach the same conclusion about another person in the same situation. [L1206.011]
  5. If there is no good reason, ask why you keep acting as if it were true. [L1206.011]
  6. Answer each question seriously and emotionally, not casually. A flash of healthy indignation can help break an old false idea. [L1206.011]
- Choose a past experience in which a result matched a belief you held about yourself:
  1. Write down what happened, including what you expected and how you reacted. [L1206.051]
  2. Explain the experience using one principle, such as self-image, a limiting belief or the role of an automatic goal. [L1206.051]
  3. Use the explanation to decide which belief to test next. [L1206.051]

  Start the procedure by choosing that experience. [L1206.051]
- Negative beliefs are usually built through repetition and emotion. [L1206.012]
- Trying to prove or disprove a new mental method through intellectual argument or endless debate prevents you from testing it. The method can only be judged by practicing it and observing your own results. [L1206.025]

### Visualization and mental rehearsal

#### Timing and daily practice

- Give any new mindset practice at least 21 days before judging whether it works, because roughly that long is needed for an old mental image to fade and a new one to settle. During this window, do not keep checking your progress or debate whether the method is valid. [L1206.003]
- During the first 21 days of a new mindset practice, review the core principles about three times a week and look for examples in your own life and in people you observe. Do not measure your progress or argue with the method during this period. The point is to test the practice, not to judge it early. [L1206.052]
- Practice visualization every day, since consistency matters more than length. Sessions of 10 to 15 minutes can produce noticeable change, and 30 minutes can be built up to once the daily habit is established. [L1206.004]
- The nervous system responds to a vividly and richly imagined experience almost as it does to a real one. Mental rehearsal can therefore build skills and confidence that carry over into action. [L1206.005]
- Spend most of your time focused on the process and the next steps toward a goal. Revisit the end result briefly, once or twice a day, during visualization, then return to the process. [L1206.016]
- Forcing the answer to how a goal will be reached, before the goal is clearly defined, blocks the mind from finding it. Define the goal first; the practical steps tend to surface when you are relaxed. [L1206.017]
- When willpower and imagination conflict, imagination usually wins. Goals should therefore be pictured calmly rather than forced through strained effort. [L1206.040]

#### Daily 30-minute visualization

1. Set aside about 30 minutes alone and undisturbed each day. [L1206.008]
2. Sit or lie down comfortably and close your eyes. [L1206.008]
3. Picture yourself on a large screen, or see the scene from your own eyes, whichever feels more vivid. [L1206.008]
4. Add small sensory details to the scene: sounds, smells, textures, objects, and the surroundings. [L1206.008]
5. Picture yourself acting, responding, and feeling in an ideal way in the situation. Ignore how you acted yesterday. [L1206.008]
6. Do not tell yourself you will act this way tomorrow. Imagine acting this way now, during the session. [L1206.008]
7. Let the emotion of already being that person come up as you watch the scene. [L1206.008]
8. If your mind wanders, return to the picture calmly without self-criticism. [L1206.008]
9. Repeat the session every day, starting with 10 to 15 minutes if 30 is too much. [L1206.008]

#### Relaxation before imagery

1. Sit comfortably, or stand. Lying down works, but it may lead to falling asleep and reduce the benefit. [L1206.009]
2. Scan your body for tension and consciously let go in the head, neck, torso, and legs. [L1206.009]
3. Smile inwardly to help the body relax. [L1206.009]
4. Breathe deeply and follow each inhale and exhale. [L1206.009]
5. Spend about five minutes relaxing with conscious effort, then stop controlling the muscles and let imagery bring on the relaxed state. [L1206.009]
6. Use one relaxation image: heavy limbs as if made of concrete sinking into a surface, a body held loosely like a marionette with slack strings, or a body deflating slowly like a balloon. [L1206.009]
7. Or recall a calm past scene in detail, noting background sounds, temperature, and textures. [L1206.009]

#### Anchoring a positive self-image

1. Relax first, using the breathing and relaxation steps. [L1206.010]
2. Recall any past occasion when you did something well. It can be trivial, such as learning a basic skill, and its size and date do not matter. [L1206.010]
3. Replay the memory in detail until it brings back a positive, pleasant feeling in the present. [L1206.010]
4. Picture yourself in a future situation the way you want to be. [L1206.010]
5. Attach the same feeling from the past memory to that future picture, adding emotion to what you see. [L1206.010]
6. If attention drifts, bring it back without judging yourself. [L1206.010]

#### Motor rehearsal

1. Form a clear mental image of the shot, movement or task you are about to perform. [L1206.036]
2. Rehearse it in your mind in full detail, including how it feels when it goes right. [L1206.036]
3. Step up and perform, relying on the practiced pattern rather than consciously controlling each movement. [L1206.036]
4. If the result misses, note the difference and adjust the mental image before the next attempt. [L1206.036]

#### Evidence from studies and cases

| Study or case | What was practiced | Result | Ids |
|---|---|---|---|
| Basketball free throws, controlled study | Imagined shooting for about 20 minutes a day over 20 days | Scoring improved 23 percent. Physical practice improved 24 percent. No practice showed no gain. | [L1206.006] |
| Basketball free throws, correction of misses | Imagined practice that included correcting each missed shot after it happened | 23 percent scoring gain over 20 days, close to the 24 percent gain from physical practice | [L1206.032] |
| Darts, controlled experiment | Sitting before a target each day and imagining throws | Accuracy improved about as much as real throwing practice, suggesting mental rehearsal can match physical rehearsal for well-defined motor skills | [L1206.033] |
| Salespeople rehearsing conversations | Imagined customer conversations and objections, with the best reply worked out for each | Sales increases of 100 to 150 percent in some groups and up to 400 percent for individuals. These figures are anecdotal, not a reliable benchmark. | [L1206.007] |

- Mental practice works best when it includes correcting errors, not only replaying successful shots. [L1206.032]
- Pitching academy: a coach taught high school and college baseball players mental imagery and relaxation exercises to help them reach 90 miles per hour. Many players could not reach it by effort alone, but many hit 90 for the first time once their bodies relaxed. Their self-images then adjusted to the new velocity, and within 18 months the number of players at the facility who broke 90 mph rose from 18 to 98. The coach's account is anecdotal and not a controlled study. [L1206.034]
- Golf: a beginner who had never played sat in an easy chair for five minutes each day and visualized the key points of a correct swing, with no practice on the course. Several days later, with no physical preparation, the beginner joined a regular foursome and shot nine holes in even par, 36. The instructor's core rule was that a clear mental picture of the correct action must come before performing it. His claim that the mental side makes up 90 percent of golf, the physical side 8 percent and the mechanical side 2 percent is an unverified estimate. [L1206.035]
- Violin: a violinist was thinking of retiring because a wrist injury made regular practice difficult. A coach asked him to play the violin in his mind without holding the instrument. A week later he gave what he considered the best concert of his life, and he dropped the idea of retiring. [L1206.037]
- Chess: a challenger reportedly prepared for a championship match by playing only in his mind for three months, after giving up smoking and drinking and doing calisthenics. He defeated a champion who had been expected to win easily. The case suggests that structured mental play can prepare a competitor for a specific contest. [L1206.048]
- Dentist fear: a visualization exercise did not work until the person added concrete sensory details, such as the smell of antiseptic, the feel of the chair's armrests, and the sight of the dentist's hands approaching. Once the imagined scene was detailed enough, it produced an effect close to real experience. Vague imagery gives a much weaker result than detailed imagery. [L1206.049]
- Interview preparation:
  1. Plan the interview in advance and list the questions that are likely to be asked. [L1206.046]
  2. Think through a clear answer to each question. [L1206.046]
  3. Rehearse the whole interview in your mind, including questions you did not prepare for. [L1206.046]
  4. Practice reacting spontaneously, because rehearsal builds confidence and the ability to adapt even when the actual questions differ. [L1206.046]

### Roles and self-view in practice

- Ignore the outside verdict and measure progress against your own standard, because the goal is a stable self-image rather than external approval. [L1206.013]
- Practice the role before the proof arrives, then collect each completed piece of work as evidence for the new identity. [L1041.034, L1041.004]

#### Further details

- Success loop: 1. Take action. 2. Collect feedback on what the action produced. 3. Correct your approach based on that feedback. 4. Take action again with the correction and repeat the cycle. [L0831.002]

- Follow the success loop described by Jim Rohn in this order: 1) take action, 2) get feedback from the result, 3) make corrections based on that feedback, 4) take more action. More action produces more feedback, which allows better-informed actions in the future. Most people stall because self-limiting thoughts stop them from taking the first action. [L1513.003]

## Know your strengths, values and working style

### Start with honest self-reflection

- Self-reflection exercises about work habits and thinking style have no right or wrong answers. Answering openly and honestly with yourself is what makes them useful. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L0802.008]
- Skipping self-reflection exercises because they feel uncomfortable reduces their value, since they are often the most useful part of personal business preparation. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L0802.007]
- Formal education, a strong academic record and exceptional intelligence are not prerequisites for building a profitable e-commerce business. Long-term persistence through slow or losing periods matters more than traditional credentials. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L0419.014]
- Persistence is the trait that carries successful sellers: checking the numbers every day, generating ideas, and running the team with genuine belief. Persistence does not mean stubbornly trying to solve everything alone. [L0780.015]

### Review your past attempts honestly

- Reviewing past attempts at online business honestly, including what happened and why it ended, reveals recurring patterns such as quitting early or lacking capital. Address those patterns in the new plan rather than repeating them. [L1503.007]
- Answer each question of this self-assessment form in writing before starting an online business:

```
Personal self-assessment form to fill in honestly before starting an online business, answer each question in writing:
1. Name, age, and home country or city?
2. What do you currently do for work or study?
3. Why did you decide to start this business path now?
4. What is your strongest reason for wanting to succeed in ecommerce?
5. What is your main goal for the next 90 days?
6. What monthly or yearly income figure are you working toward?
7. Have you tried ecommerce, dropshipping, or any other online business before? If yes, what happened and why did it stop or fail?
8. What do you see as your biggest obstacle right now? Consider time, confidence, product research, ads, capital, and consistency.
9. What do you expect from a coach, course, or mentor, and how should they support you?
10. Imagine it is 12 months from today and everything worked out. Describe in concrete detail what your work, income, time, and daily life look like.
Commitment (sign or confirm): Guidance, support, and accountability can be provided by others, but results depend on taking consistent action yourself.
```
[L1503.001]

- Answering the planning questions quickly or vaguely undermines the whole plan, because the answers are the basis for setting realistic goals. Fill in each answer with specifics such as numbers, dates, and real constraints. [L1503.006]

### Map your personality and natural strengths

- Every person has a unique way of thinking and acting that works automatically and cannot be switched off. Trying to act against it drains energy, while working inside it makes tasks feel natural. [L0701.013]
- Myers-Briggs and DISC are personality assessments that help you understand how you think and deliver value. DISC is also used to read other people in one-on-one conversations so you can choose the right words. Free resources for both are available online. [L0701.018]
- The StrengthsFinder assessment from Gallup, described in Tom Rath's book, identifies your five natural strengths or talents. The Gallup study behind it reports that people operate about 80% of their lives from these five strengths, so work should be built around them. [L0701.019]
- Books alone rarely reveal your own strengths and working style. Get feedback from other people who know you well, and combine it with structured assessments such as Gallup's StrengthsFinder. [L0701.039]
- Many people stop using their natural way of working around ages 8 to 14, when they are told that what they do by default is not enough and that they must add unrelated skills to succeed. Rebuilding confidence starts with returning to that natural way of thinking and acting, not with adding more courses. [L0701.037]

### Define values, passion and purpose

- Values work as market selection: they decide which audience you want to serve and why. When your work fits your values, it becomes more enjoyable and marketing gets easier. [L0701.017]
- Problems that you notice in others, often before they notice them, are a strong starting point for a business. Those problems are usually linked to your natural way of seeing things. [L0701.014]

### Build in the right order

Work through these steps in sequence:

1. Understand your personality and how you process information. [L0701.016]
2. Identify your proficiencies, the five natural ways you think and act. [L0701.016]
3. Define your values, which determine who you want to work with and why. [L0701.016]
4. Find your passion, the drive to endure pain for the end result. [L0701.016]
5. Define your purpose, what you offer the world. [L0701.016]
6. Make a plan based on the steps above. [L0701.016]
7. Execute. [L0701.016]

- Plans that look complete on paper but collapse in execution, with about 50% failing right away, usually mean planning was done too early. Planning should come late, after personality, strengths, values and passion are clear. [L0701.005]
- Constantly second-guessing your marketing (target group, wording, message) usually signals that you do not yet know your own strengths and values well enough to speak clearly to customers. Fix the self-understanding first, then the messaging. [L0701.004]

### Check that your work fits your strengths

- Heavy work that produces small results usually means you are working outside your proficiencies and your natural working style. Re-check which tasks match your strengths before adding more effort. [L0701.021]

### Choose partners and direction carefully

- Repeated partnerships that do not match, where the cause is never clear, often come from unclear personal strengths and values rather than bad luck. Define both before entering a partnership. [L0701.006]
- Chasing every new opportunity (shiny button syndrome) keeps you from finishing anything and drains money and focus. Stay with one direction until it is defined by your strengths and values. [L0701.007]

### Name your obstacles

- Name your obstacles specifically in categories such as time, confidence, product research, ads, capital, and consistency, and rank the biggest one. Each named obstacle can then be matched to a concrete countermeasure. [L1503.005]

### Use the experience you already have

- Management experience from a corporate job transfers well to running a business, even when the person has no prior business ownership. Those skills in planning, team leadership and handling pressure apply directly to e-commerce. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1014.001]
- Prior sales experience makes supplier contact much easier. Without it, expect the first calls to feel daunting, which is normal. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L1248.009]

#### Case: investing profits in an offline business (around 2009 to 2010)

Around 2009 to 2010, the owner invested profits from an e-commerce business into a digital signage company that installed displays in about 30 coffee shop locations in New York. The costs were very high because of computers, servers, screens, installation staff, and sales staff, and the business made no money. The owner sold it quickly, recovered only part of the cash, and concluded that offline businesses, managing many companies, and large teams were not a fit. [L1039.031]

#### Case: a low-ticket store with China-shipped products (around 2011 to 2012)

Around 2011 to 2012, the owner built a low-ticket store selling products shipped from China, with an average order value of about $50, and drove traffic to it. Customers quickly complained about long shipping times, and no support systems existed for the volume of customers, so many were upset. The products were delivered, the website was shut down, and the lesson was that this model was not for them. Orders producing about $8 profit do not justify a large support team. [L1039.032]

### Feelings of imposter syndrome

- Feelings of imposter syndrome are normal for entrepreneurs entering a new market. Push through them by taking small actions rather than waiting until you feel fully qualified. (Older advice, 2021-2022.) [L0453.017]
- The feeling of being a fraud at the start of a business usually comes from an unexplored personality. Clarifying your personality and the value you bring removes most of that feeling. [L0701.020]
- Feeling like an impostor is normal even for experienced creators, and nobody fully understands where their best ideas come from. Show up and do the work every day instead of waiting until you feel ready. [L1343.024]

### Measure your confidence

Repeat this exercise to track how your confidence changes:

1. On a sheet of paper, rate from 1 to 10 how confident you are in your ability to do your job right now. [L0701.002]
2. Below the rating, list every task you have to do (for example marketing, setting up the store). [L0701.002]
3. Keep the sheet and repeat the 1 to 10 rating after the gratitude exercise to measure the change. [L0701.002]

## Commit your time, money and effort

### Written commitment and accountability

- Before you start a training program, write a concrete action plan. It states how many hours per day and how many days per week you will study and act, rather than just deciding to finish the material quickly. [L0444.001]
- Your results depend on the actions you actually take. Without a written plan, the outcome is left to chance. Decide in advance which actions you will take and how often, so you shape your results instead of accepting whatever happens. [L0444.020]
- Write a personal pledge in a notebook. Use this wording as the template:

```text
I will put in X hours each day and work on e-commerce X days every week.
I will keep this up until I have completed the course and tested at least 12 products.
Until I have tested 12 products, I will not doubt the method.
Regardless of circumstances, I will go through the entire course and test 12 products.
Date: [today's date]
Signature: [your signature]
```
[L0444.018]

- Make the commitment official and accountable:
  1. Photograph or scan the handwritten, signed commitment. [L0444.019]
  2. If you have a business partner, each of you signs a commitment and shows it to the other. [L0444.019]
  3. If you work alone, send the signed commitment to the program's support contact by email so it is recorded and checked. [L0444.019]
- Outside support such as coaching, mentoring or a course adds value only when you take consistent action. Results come from daily execution, so accountability is your responsibility, not the mentor's. [L1503.008]

### Realistic time minimums

- Put at least one hour per day into your business. Consistent daily effort produces measurable growth and results after one month, six months and one year. [L0280.035]
- Avoid analysis paralysis by taking one concrete action every day toward your business, even while still learning. Combining action with learning is far more valuable than consuming information without acting. [L0280.036]
- Set a realistic weekly time commitment for the program: a minimum of 2 hours per day on 6 days per week, which totals 12 hours per week. If you have fewer study days, compress the same 12 hours into fewer days, but do not go below 12 hours per week. [L0444.002]
- Set a minimum effort that is realistic enough to produce results within the expected time. A very low, unrealistic minimum will probably not produce results, and a person who quits before the reasonable point never sees the outcome. [L0444.017]
- Do not set an overly ambitious daily study target, such as 12 hours per day alongside a day job. Missing an unrealistic target leads to frustration, demotivation and the false belief that the training does not work. [L0444.003]
- Consistency matters more than hours. Even 15 minutes per day adds up over time. [L0671.144, L0671.279]
- Consistency of daily effort matters more than the number of hours. Even one lesson plus its action item per day, for someone with only an evening free, can lead to a launched business within about a month, though it may take longer. [L1496.003]
- Squeezing study into very small, scattered time slots while pushing yourself hard is demotivating and raises the risk of dropping out. A daily time commitment you can sustain produces more progress. [L1496.013]
- Speed of progress does not determine long-run success. Anyone working with less time per day can be just as successful as someone working more, as long as they do the right things. [L0438.004] *(Source B, 2021-22: older advice.)*

### Time values by source

Sources give different time values. Each value applies to a different situation, as shown below.

| Time value | Situation it applies to | Source |
|---|---|---|
| At least 1 hour per day | Ongoing business work, any stage | [L0280.035] |
| 2 hours per day, 6 days per week (12 hours per week minimum) | Minimum for an e-commerce training program | [L0444.002] |
| About 1 hour per day | Building a first high-ticket dropshipping store; progress is slower. *(2021-22, older)* | [L0438.003] |
| About 4 hours per day | Building a first high-ticket dropshipping store; progress is faster. *(2021-22, older)* | [L0438.003] |
| About 30 minutes per day, 5 days per week | Keeping a store moving part-time; outsource uploads and store build when time is scarce | [L0471.050] |
| 30 to 60 minutes of focused work per day for about two months | Planning estimate for launching a store by following the training step by step. This is a time estimate only, not a guaranteed result, and passive browsing does not count. | [L0970.165] |
| 1 to 2 hours per day for about three months | A slower but valid path. Owners with 1 hour per day may need longer. *(2019, older)* | [L1110.001, L0771.057] |
| About 30 days | A launch is possible in about 30 days if the owner has enough time. Speed of launch does not predict long-term results. *(2021-22, older)* | [L0771.057] |
| 15 minutes per day | Minimum that still adds up over time | [L0671.144, L0671.279] |

- A store can be built around the time the owner has, so launch timing should match the owner's real weekly hours instead of an ideal plan. Staying realistic prevents frustration and wasted effort. [L1068.079] *(Source A, 2019: older advice.)*
- The pace of the roadmap depends on the hours per week you can dedicate. Some operators finish the early phases much faster than the standard weekly plan. [L0914.018]
- Owners who work long days on a store alongside a full-time job should lower ad budgets instead of pausing campaigns to protect their energy. Protect your health and pace the work. [L0469.111] *(Source A, 2021: older advice.)*

### Time for the day job

- Dropshipping can be run as a side business alongside a day job. Set aside time outside work hours to launch the store, then use outsourcing to reduce time spent once sales are coming in. [L0771.058] *(Source B, 2021-22: older advice.)*
- A person with a full-time job can still build a business by committing a fixed daily block, for example half an hour at the same time every day. Use the same discipline you would apply in a workplace. [L0832.006]
- Scarce money can be offset by investing personal time. Working on a business before or after a day job and into the evening is a valid way to start with no capital. [L1041.011]
- Daily time after work can be used productively: keep testing new products, create content and build the store's traffic, and study marketing and design skills. [L1077.045]
- If you still have a job, a mortgage or dependants, build the online business on the side. Leave the job only once the business produces steady income. Replacing the salary too early exposes the household to unnecessary risk. [L1014.003] *(Source B, 2021-22: older advice.)*
- If you hold a full-time job while building a store, you cannot answer every call, email or chat live. Delayed replies are a normal limit of a side business, not a failure. [L0909.003] *(Source B, 2021-22: older advice.)*
- Working on a business alongside a job lowers financial pressure but makes it easier to find excuses for skipping work. A stronger commitment comes from a clear personal reason and a fixed daily work schedule, whichever way the business is started. [L0826.027]
- Founders with no steady paycheck often push harder to make the business work. Commitment and drive matter more than free time when launching a store. [L0471.051] *(Source A, 2024.)*
- Spreading time across too many projects at once is harmful. Start new ventures conservatively with a small share of time while keeping the established activity running. [L0805.052]

### Fixed routine and workplace

- Decide when in your week you will work on the business and keep that time. The business takes time to build before it produces revenue. [L0671.143, L0671.278]
- Set a fixed office location and fixed working hours, for example 9 a.m. to 5 p.m., and treat that time as work time. A kitchen table can work, but only if it is used for business at set hours and kept quiet. [L0832.004]
- An office does not have to be a room. A coffee shop works when traveling, as long as you sit down and set working hours. The office gives a defined space and start and end times for each goal. [L0832.008]
- Working from home alone without deadlines makes focus hard. Set self-imposed structure and discipline to keep working, especially when money is already coming in. [L0690.036]
- Dropshipping can support location independence, but remote workers still need a fixed routine to stay productive. Working Monday to Friday with weekends fully off was the most effective rhythm for a remote operator. [L1441.001]
- The biggest reason beginners fail is not doing the work. Many people enroll but never log in, or log in only once. Commit to a regular schedule to make progress. [L0934.109, L0934.186]
- Do not stop partway through the setup. Without finishing the steps, no store can make a sale. [L0671.145]

### Starting capital and testing budget

- Use your own saved money, such as income from a job, savings or an inheritance, as the beginner testing budget. Avoid leveraged money such as bank loans. [L1394.007]
- Do not fund beginner testing with credit cards. Using them at this stage is a major red flag. [L1394.008]
- A large budget is not required to start. A small budget can push a beginner to work harder. Budget is rarely the reason a store fails. [L0934.111, L0934.188]
- Before launch, the main commitment in a dropshipping setup is time rather than money. Financial outlay is concentrated at the marketing stage that follows launch. [L0438.008] *(Source B, 2021-22: older advice.)*
- Business costs for a new store can be a few hundred dollars if the owner does the setup work personally. Costs rise mainly when work such as logos, site build or uploads is outsourced. [L0801.167] *(Source A, 2021: older advice.)*

### Working with limited capital

- When capital is limited, use other levers: skills, time and resources. If one lever is short, pull another, for example by learning Photoshop or Canva and filming products yourself. [L0561.045]
- Assess your position on three levers: time, money and skill.
  1. If you have money but little time, outsource tasks. [L1041.010]
  2. If you have time but little money, do the work yourself. [L1041.010]
  3. Build skills in every case. When stuck, analyze your current situation to find a lever you can pull. [L1041.010]
- Starting capital example: about 300 pounds was enough to build a store and run a first Facebook ad of 45 pounds. Ads can be tested from as little as five pounds per day. [L0561.046]

### Staffing and outsourcing in the early phase

- A store with a few suppliers still needs daily time for more approvals and uploads, even after launch. Expect this. [L0836.058] *(Source A, 2024.)*
- When time is scarce, outsource product uploads and store build, and oversee the outsourced work. Consistent part-time work can keep a store moving. [L0471.050] *(Source A, 2024.)*
- Without budget for staff, the founder does customer service, supplier contact and fulfilment personally at first. Calling suppliers before they close for the day, even very early in the morning, can be necessary when time zones are far apart. This phase ends when the store is profitable enough to hire help. [L0910.005]

### Buffers for the unknown and timelines

- Beginners underestimate the hurdles of a first business. Include a buffer for unknown difficulties in every time estimate, and plan slower than feels necessary to avoid burnout. [L0444.005]
- Building a sustainable, profitable store can take months. The pace depends on how fast the owner works through tasks. A slower path still beats quitting, because stopping guarantees no results. Treat the work as delayed gratification: effort now produces returns later. [L1110.001] *(Source A, 2019: older advice.)*
- A lead generation machine needs time in three parts:
  - 10 to 20 hours to write or outsource the lead magnet. [L1076.022]
  - 1 to 2 hours for the lead page. [L1076.022]
  - 5 to 10 hours to build the flow. [L1076.022]
  - Maximum total: about 20 to 30 hours. Let it run for one to two months before judging the results. [L1076.022]
- Stay with the commitment until the outcome is visible. Stay focused only on tasks that move the business forward, then commit to your goals and make a personal commitment to do whatever it takes to reach them. [L0419.008] *(Source B, 2021-22: older advice.)*
- Apply what you learn every day, or whenever your schedule allows, even with a full-time job. Small steps count as long as you keep taking them consistently. [L0419.007] *(Source B, 2021-22: older advice.)*
- The most common reason beginners fail is not doing the work. Many people enroll but never log in, or log in only once. A large budget is not needed to start, and a small budget can push a beginner to work harder. [L0934.109, L0934.186, L0934.111, L0934.188]

## Set up your environment, health and daily routines

### Environment before willpower

- Willpower is limited per day, so do not rely on self-control. Remove temptations and distractions such as unhealthy food, games or negative people from your surroundings. [L1041.014]
- Remove negative influences and obstacles from your environment, organise it to conserve resources, treat yourself well and reward progress. This is what makes behavioural change sustainable. [L0007.011]

### Content and feed curation

- Choose content deliberately to support your goals, and stop consuming content that does not serve them. Curate your own feed and teach the algorithm what you want to see. This takes practice but compounds. [L0007.012]
- Control the content you consume, because it shapes your perception and, through it, your reality. Choose educational and motivational content that supports your goals, and train the social media algorithm by watching the content you want to see. [L1041.018]
- Use social media for business only, and avoid personal content on business accounts. This approach works as an experiment and is not mandatory. (Source B, 2021-2022, older advice.) [L0526.005]
- Why limit follows: a feed filled with thousands of followed accounts makes relevant business posts less likely to appear, because the platform limits how much content it shows and fills space with ads that cannot be removed. Limiting follows keeps business content visible. (Source B, 2021-2022, older advice.) [L0526.004]

**Set up social media for business use only** (Source B, 2021-2022, older advice):

1. Turn off all social media notifications, except those from Dream 100 groups. [L0526.003]
2. Follow only Dream 100 members on the platforms. Do not follow friends, family or others, unless a partner or close person would otherwise be upset (a judgement call). [L0526.003]
3. Join only groups that are Dream 100 groups or groups you manage for your business. [L0526.003]
4. Interact only with your business pages. [L0526.003]
5. Keep the feed limited to business-relevant content, so that the business connections you need are not crowded out by other posts. [L0526.003]

### Focus and work environment

- Practise mindfulness: focus on one task at a time and observe the present. This reduces anxiety and sharpens focus, and it can be used during set periods of the day without daily meditation. [L1041.022]
- Reduce distractions during focused work with headphones playing calm music or white noise, or by working in a coffee-shop-type environment. Noise-cancelling headphones are a worthwhile investment for people with children or noisy homes. [L1041.023]
- Work from a co-working space or a café with other people working. This makes it harder to drift into home distractions. [L0826.009]

### Sleep

| Item | Value | Units |
|---|---|---|
| Minimum sleep per night | 7 hours | L1181.013 |
| Ideal sleep per night | 8 hours | L1181.013 |

- Aim for a consistent amount of sleep rather than extreme sleep schedules, and avoid oversleeping. [L1181.013]
- Sleep totals matter more than the clock. A late-night work session followed by a later wake-up is acceptable if total sleep is sufficient and productivity is high in the period when you work best. [L1181.014]
- Do not sacrifice sleep to build a store. Consistent daily action is more sustainable than working through the night. (Source A, 2021, older advice.) [L0671.146, L0671.280]
- **When the sources seem to conflict:** the late-night allowance in L1181.014 applies only when total sleep still reaches the target in L1181.013 (7 hours minimum, 8 ideal). The warning in L0671.146 and L0671.280 applies whenever working late would cut into that sleep. [L1181.013, L1181.014, L0671.146, L0671.280]

### Exercise and physical health

- Exercise every day. Base the decision to train on prior commitment, not on how you feel that morning. Discipline matters more than daily mood. [L0705.004]
- The type of training is flexible (gym, sports, cardio, pilates or similar). The essential requirement is that it becomes part of the daily routine. [L0705.005]
- Regular exercise raises self-esteem and self-confidence, improves the ability to complete difficult tasks, and builds physical and especially mental endurance. [L0705.006]
- Gym sessions of 30 minutes to 1 hour fit into the daily structure after waking. [L1181.003]
- Include a gym session in the morning routine. [L1041.031]
- Physical fitness supports the energy, focus and long working hours a business needs. Getting tired after only one or two hours of sitting work is a warning sign about health and should not be treated as normal. [L0826.010]
- Physical activity helps thinking. Combine work with movement, such as standing, walking or handling materials. Long stretches of sitting still at a screen reduce both energy and output. [L1343.016]
- Protect your health as part of the work: sleep well, eat regular meals, exercise and get medical check-ups, because creative work needs energy. Harmful habits reduce long-term output. [L1343.031]

### Food

- Cook your own food and pay closer attention to what you eat. Food is the fuel for your body, and you must live with its effects for the rest of your life. [L0705.010]
- Avoid fast food, frozen food, fried items and sugar. They make you feel worse after meals compared with healthy meals. [L0705.011]
- If you cannot cook all your own meals, home-cooked food prepared by family members still counts as a healthy diet habit. The aim is meals made from fresh ingredients at home rather than fast food or frozen products. [L0705.015]

### Reading

- Read at least 30 minutes of non-fiction every day. Choose books that build financial and business knowledge, and avoid light entertainment reading. [L0705.001]

### Meditation, mindset and personal development

- Meditation is strongly recommended as a daily practice in a fixed, pre-blocked slot of 5, 10 or 30 minutes. Ideally do it right after waking to start the day with more mental clarity. [L0705.008]
- Set a timer for the planned session length, for example 20 minutes. Ideally the session is in total silence, although the speaker used music to help. [L0705.009]
- Choose a practice time you will actually keep. Morning and bedtime are common favourites, but a lunch break or scheduled break is far better than skipping the practice altogether. [L1206.031]
- Practise relaxation during the day by briefly recalling the physical feeling of relaxation several times, for example by mentally repeating that you feel more relaxed. Doing this several times a day can reduce fatigue and improve how you handle situations. [L1206.030]
- For a solo owner in the early stage, business performance depends heavily on the owner's mental and physical condition. If the owner is not in a good state, the chances of getting the business running successfully drop significantly, so personal development belongs alongside business building. (Source B, 2021-2022, older advice.) [L1256.001]

**Daily mindset routine** (Source B, 2021-2022, older advice):

1. Sit calmly and silently repeat the mantra: *I am focused, I am committed, and I am patient.* [L0419.013]
2. While keeping your attention on reaching your goals, say: *I am focused on reaching my goals, I am committed to my goals, and I have the patience to reach my goals.* [L0419.013]

### Cold showers

**Cold shower routine for extra energy** (optional):

1. Take a normal warm shower. [L0705.007]
2. Switch to cold water for the last 2 to 3 minutes. Alternatively, wash only with the coldest water available. [L0705.007]
3. The speaker's own practice was once or twice per week, after cardio. It can be done every morning when extra energy is needed. [L0705.007]

### Daily routines

**Fixed routine**

- Wake up at the same time each day and leave home for a set workplace at the same time. [L0826.009]
- Keep a fixed daily routine when travelling or working from home, with set work hours and set family time. Mixing too much work into family time, or the reverse, throws the balance off. [L1000.015]
- Keep a fixed daily routine with a regular work slot, and avoid breaking it. Inertia stops progress and makes the work harder to restart. The aim is to do the work every day, even when it does not feel productive. [L1343.028]
- Systems thinking applies to health and relationships. Fixed workout times, a consistent sleep schedule and scheduled time with a partner produce more consistent energy and results than leaving them to chance. [L1039.023]

**Morning routine for setting up the day** (start before about 8:00):

1. Take a 30-minute walk while reading the one-year and five-year goal lists. [L1041.031]
2. Do a gym session. [L1041.031]
3. Review business metrics and finances. [L1041.031]
4. Block focused work time in the calendar before opening the task list. [L1041.031]

**Daily structure used by the speaker:**

1. Wake up around 5:00-6:00 a.m. [L1181.003]
2. First hour: no phone, notifications off, focus mode. Use it for mindset preparation: coffee, a 30-minute walk in daylight, and reviewing goals and the kind of person you want to be. [L1181.003]
3. Gym for 30 minutes to 1 hour. [L1181.003]
4. Shower and get ready. [L1181.003]
5. Start work around 7:30 a.m. with the business review: emails, finances, ad performance and order checks. [L1181.003]
6. Spend about 30 minutes on messages from students or the community. [L1181.003]
7. Work on ads for the first two hours after business and community tasks. [L1181.003]
8. Afternoon: handle ad hoc tasks such as student calls, mentoring work and strategy preparation. Book them into the calendar as they come up. [L1181.003]

### Breaks and burnout prevention

- Sustained motivation requires planned rest. Schedule breaks and avoid constant overwork to prevent burnout, and end each break with a defined next goal. [L0826.026]
- Rest is part of productivity. A short 15-30 minute break with quiet music and no screens, such as a power nap in the early afternoon, can reset energy levels when the body is tired. [L1181.009]

## Build the goal ladder and financial targets

### Define your goals before you expect results

- Define your own goal before you expect results, because the time you need depends on it. [L0438.013] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- A side income that supplements a main income is a much smaller target than building a large business with millions in annual revenue. [L0438.013] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Set a financial goal for the business and a personal goal for life outside it, then use both to guide decisions about the store and how much time to invest:
  1. Set a financial goal for the business.
  2. Set a personal goal for life outside the business.
  3. Use both goals to guide decisions about the store and how much time to invest. [L0345.006]
- Start thinking about next year's goals before the year begins, and set the yearly plan in advance by breaking long-term goals into steps. [L0472.067]

### Write a vision and build the goal ladder backwards

- Start business planning with a written vision document that describes the business in 10 years. Derive the 3-year and 1-year goals from it and record them in a tracking document. [L0441.002] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- The 3-year and 1-year goals should be checked against the 10-year vision and can change as progress is made. [L0441.002] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- A 10-year vision is a destination. Planning every step to it in detail far in advance is unrealistic because the online business space changes quickly. Break the path into 3-year and 1-year milestones so that strategy and tactics are easier to plan. [L1519.001] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Build the roadmap with three horizons: a 10-year vision, a 3-year goal and a 1-year goal. Plan the 3-year and 1-year goals in less detail than the 10-year vision. [L1519.002] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- For each horizon (10 years, 3 years, 1 year), define four primary elements:
  - a revenue goal;
  - a profit goal, stated either as a dollar amount or as a margin percentage;
  - the business measurables needed to reach those numbers;
  - a short, one-paragraph description of what the business looks like at that point. [L1519.003] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Plan backwards from the 10-year vision:
  1. Decide the 3-year revenue goal, the profit goal, the measurables and a short business description, based on where you think you should be. It is acceptable to be experimental.
  2. Split the 10-year revenue target into roughly equal steps. For example, 10 million dollars in 10 years can be reached through 3 million dollars in 3 years if growth is linear.
  3. Repeat the same questions for the 1-year goal, working back from the 3-year goal. Growth does not need to be linear, but the steps help set a realistic path. [L1519.005] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Filling in the 10-year vision, 3-year and 1-year goals without working backwards from the 10-year target risks setting unrelated numbers. Each shorter-term goal should visibly move toward the longer-term one. [L1519.017] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Source A sets business goals on four horizons: 10-year, 3-year, 12-month and one-month. Personal life planning runs on a parallel track of 10-year, 3-year and 12-year plans. The source reads "12-year" for the personal track; given the 12-month horizon above, this is most likely a typo for 12-month. [L1437.036] (Source A, undated)
- Source D uses a 90-day horizon for the main goal and a 12-month horizon for the long-term vision when setting personal targets for an ecommerce business. [L1503.002] (Source D 2026)
- The sources differ on horizon length. Use the 10/3/1-year ladder for long-range business planning, and the 90-day main goal with a 12-month vision for near-term personal targets. [L1519.002, L1503.002, L1437.036]

### Set revenue and profit targets

- Revenue and profit targets are a starting point and are expected to be revised, because the relationship between revenue and profit is not known at the beginning. Set them early anyway, since they give direction to work backwards from. [L1246.023] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Define financial targets for the business as follows:
  1. Add to your written vision a specific revenue target and a specific profit target for the store.
  2. If you plan to exit (sell) the business, write down a specific target exit payout, for example 50,000, 100,000, 500,000, 1,000,000 or 5,000,000 dollars.
  3. Research the typical sale multiples that dropshipping businesses are currently sold for, using other websites or community groups.
  4. Work backwards from the target exit payout and the multiple to a required revenue or profit goal.
  5. Estimate how long reaching these goals would take and write down the ideal outcome. [L1246.014] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- A six-figure sum is not necessarily permanent life-changing money, because it may not generate enough income to live from the interest. Set targets high enough to allow lasting financial independence. [L1246.015] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Source D describes the first milestone as a benchmark: the difference between students who waste six months and students who reach their first 10,000 dollars per month in revenue. [L1452.014] (Source D 2026)
- Before considering the first roadmap complete, check these end results:
  1. A fully launched branded dropshipping store.
  2. A validated product or system generating consistent sales.
  3. The knowledge to scale beyond 10,000 dollars per month toward six figures. [L0914.020] (Source D 2026)

#### Scale milestones from the sources

Sources give different figures for different stages. Use the stage that matches your store.

| Stage | Target | Source | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Beginner, first goal | 3,000 USD profit, about 2 months ahead | [L0826.002] | Source A, undated |
| Store falls short | Reset to 2,000 USD net profit per month, make it profitable, then build a second store | [L0962.140] | Source A 2021, older advice |
| Realistic six-month target | 4,000 USD net profit per month within six months, with good price points and a clear plan | [L0962.142] | Source A 2021, older advice |
| Next goal after early success | 5,000 USD profit per month, or selling the existing store | [L0826.016] | Source A, undated |
| First revenue milestone | 10,000 USD per month in revenue | [L1452.014, L0914.020] | Source D 2026 |
| Large business | 1 million USD revenue in a year, 300,000 USD profit | [L1519.016] | Source B 2021-22, older advice; example figures |

- Source B's 10-year example of 10 million dollars annual revenue is a large-business target and does not apply to a beginner store. [L1519.012, L0438.013] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)

### Make goals specific and measurable

- Write goals with a specific target date and a specific profit figure. Vague aims such as having a successful store or making a sale cannot be measured and are easy to abandon. [L0826.001] (Source A, undated)
- Write a concrete income target and a 12-month vision. Vague goals such as wanting more money are hard to track and easy to abandon. [L1503.004] (Source D 2026)
- Turn a goal into an executable plan in this order:
  1. Set a specific, realistic target date. For a beginner, about two months ahead.
  2. Set a concrete profit target. For a beginner, 3,000 USD profit.
  3. Write the specific actions needed to reach that target, not only the wish.
  4. Break each action into a countable number with a deadline. For example, 5 supplier approvals obtained by calling 10 suppliers.
  5. Set daily or weekly activity targets for each action. For example, calling suppliers every day for two weeks.
  6. Track each count and add accountability, such as sharing progress with someone, so that progress stays visible. [L0826.002] (Source A, undated)
- Specific steps combined with accountability make a goal more likely to be achieved. The more concrete the actions and the more they are committed to, the higher the chance of success. [L0826.004] (Source A, undated)
- Set a few measurable business goals at the start of the year, such as a fixed percentage increase in net profit or a monthly profit figure. Write them down with a check-in date. At each check-in, record which changes moved the number and which did not, so the next decision is based on results instead of repetition. [L0807.001] (Source A 2025)
- Detailed, specific goals are more likely to be reached than vague ones, and they should be visible to both the owner and the team. A short-term target such as 10 sales in 5 days can be reached by the end of a month. [L1437.038] (Source A, undated)
- Track these metrics and turn them into measurable targets with rewards:

| Metric | Example target |
|---|---|
| Daily sales | 3 sales per day |
| Monthly sales | $10,000 in one month |
| Conversion rate | 2 buyers per 100 website visitors (2%) |
| Organic search traffic | A set number of visitors from search |
| Phone call close ratio | 5 sales from 10 calls (50%) |
| Supplier approvals | Reaching a gold-level supplier status |

  [L1039.024] (Source A, undated)
- Define success for each store upfront, such as a monthly net profit goal over a set timeframe. A store that hits a smaller but steady net profit with minimal daily work can still be a success. [L1111.172] (Source A 2020, older advice)

### Sequence growth and expansion

- Growth should come from keeping the store focused and expanding in steps, not from launching many products or stores at once. Validate each step with traffic and sales before moving to the next. [L1114.064] (Source A 2023)
- Speed and impatience are different things. Move fast on execution while planning long-term goals in a macro view. A goal set for one to three years can be broken into a three-month plan. [L1038.006] (Source D 2026)
- When results are steady, growth requires taking larger risks. Enjoying a break is fine, but follow it by setting a bigger goal. [L0826.022] (Source A, undated)
- Set a clear next goal before starting a new project, such as reaching 5,000 USD profit per month or selling the existing store. This prevents coasting after early success. [L0826.016] (Source A, undated)

### Plan monthly and daily actions

- Break a yearly revenue goal into monthly goals. Track the current month's goal on a checklist, such as a Trello board. Review progress monthly so that you can spot where you are stuck early. [L0808.054] (Source A 2026)
- Set an internal deadline a few days before your real goal date, so that delays do not push the real goal back. [L0966.100] (Source A 2025)
- Use the goal tracking horizons below. Source A's tracking structure uses shorter horizons than his business ladder. [L1039.012, L1043.006] (Source A, undated)

| Horizon | Time frame | Content |
|---|---|---|
| Long-term | 1 to 5 years | Big life targets from the dreamline |
| Mid-term | 3 to 6 months | Stretch goals that move toward the long-term targets |
| Short-term | Monthly | Tasks this month that advance the mid-term goals |
| Daily | Each day you plan to work | Concrete tasks written the night before |

  [L1039.012] (Source A, undated)

| Horizon | Timeframe |
|---|---|
| Long-term goals | 1-5 years |
| Mid-term goals | 3-6 months |
| Short-term goals | This month |
| Daily goals | What must be done today |

  [L1043.006] (Source A, undated)

### Use the goal tracker

- Fill in the goal tracker in this order:
  1. Write the vision document describing the business in 10 years.
  2. Enter the 10-year picture in the tracker: date, revenue, profit, measurables and a brief overview taken from the vision document.
  3. Enter the 3-year picture, working backwards from the 10-year figures: date, revenue, profit, measurables and a brief overview.
  4. Enter the 1-year plan, working backwards from the 3-year figures: date, revenue, profit, the same measurables and a brief overview.
  5. Review the three horizons together and check that each shorter-term figure clearly moves toward the longer-term one. [L1519.020] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Fill in the 10-year picture of the tracker:
  1. Enter the date that is 10 years from today.
  2. Enter the revenue target in dollars, for example 10 million dollars per year.
  3. Enter the profit goal either as a dollar amount or as a margin percentage.
  4. Enter the business measurables you expect at that point, such as revenue, profit, customer numbers, cost per acquisition, repeat business rate, average order value and number of products sold.
  5. Paste in the overview of the business from the first paragraph of your vision document. Keep it to a brief overview, not the full plan. [L1519.012] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Fill in the 3-year picture of the tracker:
  1. Enter the date that is 3 years from today.
  2. Enter the revenue target, for example 3 million dollars.
  3. Enter the profit goal as a dollar amount or margin percentage.
  4. Define the measurables you need to track, making each one specific and clearly defined.
  5. Write a brief overview of what the business looks like at that point, covering the highlights, such as team size. Example: a team of 20 people in one year could mean a team of 10 staff within a few years. [L1519.013] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Fill in the 1-year plan of the tracker:
  1. Enter the date that is one year from today.
  2. Enter the revenue target for the year, for example 1 million dollars.
  3. Enter the profit goal for the year, for example 300,000 dollars, or a margin percentage.
  4. Use the same measurables as in the 3-year picture, since this is generally easiest, and set the figures for the one-year horizon, for example 1,000 customers per year.
  5. Write a brief overview of what the business should look like one year from now. [L1519.016] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)

### Set personal life goals

- Track progress against lifestyle outcomes, such as reaching a home, a trip or a car, rather than only against profit. Concrete targets motivate far more than a monthly profit figure. Without a known amount and purpose, results tend to be random. [L1039.013] (Source A, undated)
- The sources frame profit differently. Source A's personal track uses lifestyle targets, while the store-level figures above are profit targets. Use both: the store goal governs business decisions, and the lifestyle goal governs how much time you invest. [L1039.013, L0345.006] (Source A, undated)
- Stretch goals gradually. Aim somewhat beyond what you currently believe is possible, but not so far that they set you up for failure. Expand your comfort zone one step at a time so the larger goals become reachable over time. [L1039.038] (Source A, undated)
- Set goals slightly bigger than what seems possible, but realistic enough to be reached. Define the final destination before planning the path toward it. [L1043.005] (Source A, undated)

### Review, share and raise goals

- At the end of each month, sit down with the team and follow these steps:
  1. Review financial goals.
  2. Review operational goals, including return rates and the number of customer complaints.
  3. Set specific, detailed targets for the next period, including small short-term ones. [L1437.037] (Source A, undated)
- Share the goals tracker with the team as the business grows. Employees who understand and buy into the vision tend to work toward it more effectively. If the revenue and goal figures feel uncomfortable to share, that is usually a sign the goals are ambitious enough. [L1519.010] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Goals are living documents. Review and update them as the business grows. If a 3-year goal is reached early, raise it, and revise the 10-year vision upward as well. [L1519.007] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Never hitting a goal is acceptable when goals are continually raised, as long as the chase keeps pushing the business forward. Goals do not have to stay fixed once they are set. [L1519.008] (older advice, Source B 2021-22)
- Success is not final. After reaching a goal, reevaluate it, set new long-term targets and keep optimizing your procedures. Failure is not permanent either; treat it as a lesson to apply the next time. [L1039.034] (Source A, undated)

## Review the past period

### Timing and frequency

| Interval | Rule | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Annual, ideally in January | Review the past year before setting goals and a plan for the coming year (older advice, 2021-22). | L0800.001 |
| Six-monthly | The same review can be repeated every six months (older advice, 2021-22). | L0800.001 |
| Quarterly | The same review can be repeated every quarter (older advice, 2021-22). A quarterly review is also set when new Rocks are defined. | L0800.001, L0441.007 |
| Shortest allowed interval | Do not review more often than once a quarter (older advice, 2021-22). | L0800.002 |

Where the sources differ, the annual review is the baseline, and six-monthly or quarterly reviews are optional add-ons. The quarterly minimum applies to every interval. [L0800.001, L0800.002, L0441.007]

- Set a recurring calendar time for a quarterly review, held when new Rocks are defined, and for an annual review, when the business and its progress toward the yearly goals are assessed. [L0441.007]
- At the annual review, check whether the 10-year vision and the 3-year goals still fit, revise them if needed, then set the goals for the next year. [L0441.007]
- After the first 1-year plan, the next plan is a 2-year plan. [L0441.007]
- Quarterly reviews become more useful as the business grows and more changes happen in shorter periods (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.002]
- Most growth actions need longer than a quarter to show results. Shorter review periods make it hard to judge what worked, and the time spent reviewing outweighs the benefit (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.002]
- Start the review as soon as the period closes instead of postponing it to the end of the month. Complete it before you build the plan for the next period (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.004]
- At the start of your planning period, review your business performance and results from the previous year, or from the months you were in business, to establish a baseline for improvement. [L0223.004]

### Preparing the review

- Use a spreadsheet annual review template, available as an Excel version for Windows or a Numbers version for Mac (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.008]
- The template has a business half and a personal half. Each has the same seven questions: top goals, goals reached, what prevented goals, biggest weaknesses, unexploited opportunities, achievements you are proud of, and what you would have done differently (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.008]
- The questions stay the same whatever the review interval (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.008]
- Answer both the business and the personal section, since they reflect each other. Answers from one section can belong in the other, and that overlap is acceptable (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.009]
- Limit each list to a maximum of five items, and never give one-word answers. Each line should be a complete statement with enough detail to explain what happened and why (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.013]

### Mindset for the review

- Review the year objectively and without self-blame. Mistakes, failed experiments and missed goals are normal in every business, and the purpose of the review is to find what to change, not to judge yourself (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.005]
- Learning systematically from past results is a defining trait of successful business owners, whether the previous year went well or poorly. The review turns past experience into the basis for changes in the next period (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.003]
- Many people never change their situation because they do not analyze what they did and what did not work. Deliberately reviewing actions is what separates operators who improve from those who repeat the same mistakes (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.006]
- Goals are targets to aim for, not a pass or fail test. Count progress toward a goal as a useful result even when the goal was not fully reached (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.014]
- A missed goal does not make you a failure. It shows what to keep working on in the coming period (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.021]
- Achievements can be listed even if you missed all your goals. Recognizing your wins is an important part of the review (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.018]
- In a small or medium business, the owner's own performance strongly drives business results, so the review must cover both the business and the owner personally. Ignoring personal needs and personal performance puts the business at risk (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.007]

### Business section

Procedure, in this order (older advice, 2021-22):

1. Write up to five top business goals you had for the past year, one answer per line, in complete sentences. Fewer than five is fine. [L0800.011]
2. List which of these business goals you reached. Leave the answer blank if you reached none. [L0800.011]
3. For each business goal, write one line on what prevented you from reaching it. Your own assessment may or may not be exactly right; the point is to think about the reason. [L0800.011]
4. List the biggest weaknesses in the business that stopped you from reaching your top goals. Write one line per weakness, up to five. [L0800.011]
5. List the unexploited business opportunities you identified during the year, even if they overlap with your weaknesses. [L0800.011]
6. List the business achievements you are most proud of in the past year. Include progress and firsts even if you missed your goals. [L0800.011]
7. List what you would have done differently in the business. Aim for as many items as you can think of, and revisit the list over several days. [L0800.011]

Notes on the business section:

- Examples of achievements worth listing: the first month with revenue above 100,000 in your currency; organic visits growing from about 500 to 2,500 per month; hiring the first virtual assistant; launching a first business; or taking consistent steps toward launching one (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.017]
- List unexploited opportunities even if you never acted on them. They often reflect your weaknesses, so listing them alongside weaknesses is fine (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.016]

### Personal section

Procedure, in this order (older advice, 2021-22):

1. Write up to five top personal goals for the past year. They can be non-business goals such as family time, fitness, health, or personal money targets, and they do not need to be recorded before. [L0800.012]
2. List which of these personal goals you reached. Leave the answer blank if you reached none. [L0800.012]
3. For each personal goal, write one line on what prevented you from reaching it. [L0800.012]
4. List your biggest personal weaknesses and bad habits, or the areas where you feel you could have performed better in life or in your work. Write up to five. [L0800.012]
5. List the unexploited personal opportunities you identified during the year. [L0800.012]
6. List the personal achievements you are most proud of, including anything reached such as weight targets, distance targets or new skills learned. [L0800.012]
7. List what you would have done differently personally in the past year. [L0800.012]

Notes on the personal section:

- Valid personal goals to review include spending time with family, fitness and health, starting a business, earning a personal income target from the business, buying a property, or investing in shares (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.020]
- Personal weaknesses are often the hardest to identify, so give that question extra time. Negative findings in the review point to the opportunities for the coming year (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.022]

### What you would have done differently (both sections)

- For this question, spend more than five minutes and list as many items as you can (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.019]
- Include tried actions that failed and things you planned but never did (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.019]
- Do this over several days, and come back after a break to find new items (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.019]

### Turning the review into the next plan

- Use the answers from the review as the input for planning the next year, quarter or six-month period, because the weaknesses and opportunities identified here drive what gets planned (older advice, 2021-22). [L0800.010]
- Before building the plan, review the year behind you. List opportunities you have not fully exploited and identify your weaknesses (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.007]
- Use these findings to set goals, and make sure at least one weakness is fixed within the six months (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.007]
- Knowing your numbers is not enough by itself. Profit gains come only when you act on what the reports show, even on tasks that are not exciting. Set the next year's priorities from the analysis, not from sales targets alone (older advice, 2021-22). [L1046.048]
- Schedule a reset period, free of operational tasks, to review goals, business plans and new ideas from a high-level view. A virtual assistant who runs day-to-day tasks makes this possible. [L1000.020]

Turning results into routine changes:

1. After each result, decide whether it was a success or a weakness. [L1039.030]
2. If an action worked, strengthen it so it becomes part of your routine. [L1039.030]
3. If you spot a weakness, optimize that part of your routine before the next cycle. [L1039.030]

## Write the 180-day plan

### Why a written plan matters

- Business goals are rarely reached by luck or disorganized action. A written plan gives daily to-do lists their direction and reduces wasted time, procrastination and lack of motivation (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.003]
- Write down a business plan before starting, and treat it as a guide rather than a fixed rule. [L0829.018]
- Following scattered online marketing tips without a structured foundation leads to poor results, because tips do not show what matters first or how channels connect. A structured plan that sets numbers first and campaign types in order works better. [L1203.029]

### Planning horizon and timing

- Plan the next 180 days (about six months) rather than a full year, because a long horizon in an uncertain environment makes forecasts unreliable (older advice, 2021-22). Review the plan at the halfway point and adjust course if conditions have changed significantly (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.001]
- Plan in January, after the year's review, when a high-ticket dropshipping business is often quieter. This makes it easier to look ahead and check whether the business is on track (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.024]
- Time is the most valuable resource, so spend it on what makes the most money. Plan the next three months carefully, because they determine the following year. [L0334.054]
- In a compressed launch plan for a new branded dropshipping store, product research and validation can start in week 2 and overlap with store setup, because the research step can take only a few days. Ads and testing frameworks then begin around weeks 3 to 4, and the total timeline depends on how many hours per week the operator can give the project. [L0914.021]

### Setting priorities

- Limit goals and priorities to a maximum of five, because more than five are unlikely to be pursued seriously. Priorities should be major business changes for the year, not small recurring chores (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.010]
- Typical six-month priorities include growing organic traffic, setting up an email marketing system, or getting 10 new suppliers approved and products uploaded (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.011]
- Big business changes such as email marketing, SEO or widening the product range take a long time, so commit to them consistently even when distractions or new opportunities appear (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.005]
- Outsourcing should be one of the priorities in a 180-day plan, and owners should check whether their plan already includes the tasks they need to hand off (older advice, 2021-22). [L1247.001]
- If you have fewer than five priorities, fill only the matching Priority task columns and leave the rest empty (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.025]

### Task breakdown

- Example SEO task breakdown for six months: write 12 blog posts, promote those posts, run a link-building method on six of them, rewrite collection descriptions, run an SEO audit and fix duplicate content (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.015]
- Use the plan as the daily reference: the top priority and its associated tasks become the day's to-do list, so you do not need to decide each morning what matters most (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.017]

### Writing the plan

- Planning the 180 days can take ten minutes or a day or two. Finish it before starting the next task, and do not overthink it: get the goals and tasks onto the page first (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.023]

### Planner sheet and tools

- The planning tool is a Google Sheets pair: a 180-day plan sheet and a goals and rocks tracker holding the quarter tabs. It follows the Course B structure and mirrors a 10-year, 3-year, 1-year and 90-day rocks planner (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.020]
- The planner sheet has these sections and fields (older advice, 2021-22):

| Sheet | Fields |
|---|---|
| BSGR 180 Day Plan | Date (plan end and next review date); Revenue; Profit; 180 Day Goals/Priorities (no more than 5); Measurables; Paint The Picture; Priority 1 Tasks to Priority 5 Tasks |
| 90 DAY ROCKS - 1st Quarter | 90 Day Goals/Priorities; 90 Day Rocks (up to 7 rocks) |
| 90 DAY ROCKS - 2nd Quarter | 90 Day Goals/Priorities; 90 Day Rocks (up to 7 rocks) |

[L0447.019]

- For the second half of the year, duplicate the original planner and change its quarter tabs to third and fourth quarter instead of editing the first-half version (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.022]

### Checking earlier plans

- Before writing a new 180-day plan, check any earlier long-range plan (10-year, 3-year, one-year and 90-day rocks). Copy across the items that still hold and revise the ones that no longer match the business (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.021]

### Holding the plan

- Do not change the plan during its term. It stays fixed until the six-month end date, and only then is a new plan written (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.018]
- Once a plan is set, follow it through to its end without changing it mid-way, unless a major personal event occurs. Change course only at the scheduled review (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.004]

### Review date in the calendar

- Fix the review date now by scheduling a full day in the calendar roughly six months ahead to review the past period and decide what must change based on national or international conditions (older advice, 2021-22). [L0447.002]

### The planning cycle

1. Plan your business priorities and goals for the next 180 days (six months). [L0223.009]
2. Schedule a review date on your calendar for approximately six months ahead, around mid-year (June or July). [L0223.009]
3. On that date, review what you actually accomplished against your plan. [L0223.009]
4. Use the same planning tool to create your next 180-day plan based on the results and learnings from the previous period. [L0223.009]

## Set 90-day Rocks and measurables

### Why a 90-day plan matters

- Success in dropshipping comes from building systems and staying consistent, not from testing random products. A structured 90-day roadmap gives clarity and focus to move from beginner to consistent revenue. [L0199.001]
- Older advice (2021-22): Rocks are the priorities for the next 90 days and come from the goals set for the business. [L0441.003]
- Older advice (2021-22): Rocks are 90-day goals that break a one-year target into quarterly chunks. Each quarter, ask which goals must be hit to reach the one-year target. [L0679.001]
- Older advice (2021-22): Rocks give the business momentum and traction, so the owner feels progress even when growth is slower than hoped. Long periods without visible momentum lead to stagnation, self-doubt and dislike of the business, and recovering from that gets harder over time. Setting Rocks is therefore a proactive way to avoid that spiral. [L0679.002]

### Writing a valid Rock

- Older advice (2021-22): Every Rock must be specific, attainable and measurable, and it must be reviewed every 90 days. [L0679.003]
- A goal that is neither specific nor measurable, such as *more profit next quarter*, is not a valid Rock. [L0679.003]
- Example of a valid Rock: *Make an additional $60,000 profit in the next 90 days*. It is specific and measurable, so the result can be checked easily. A vague goal about more profit fails both tests. [L0679.004]
- Keep Rocks realistic for the 90-day window. People tend to overestimate what they can achieve in a short period and underestimate what they can achieve over a long period. A business with $100,000 in total sales to date should not set a Rock of $1,000,000 in one quarter. [L0679.005]
- Set no more than 7 Rocks per quarter, because more goals make focus difficult. [L0679.021]
- Use Rocks only for big-impact opportunities, such as:
  - a new product
  - promoting an existing bestseller
  - a new traffic source
  - an email marketing system
  - a hiring plan
  - a new business [L0679.021]
- Do not use Rocks for routine tasks, such as answering emails. [L0679.021]

### Breaking a one-year target into measurables

Older advice (2021-22). Worked chain:

1. One-year measurable: 600,000 extra organic search visitors. [L0679.023]
2. 90-day measurable: first publish 10 new pieces of content. [L0679.023]
3. Each content piece becomes a Rock, such as hiring a writer or writing one piece every two weeks. [L0679.023]
4. Weekly to-do lists carry these steps: in week one, write the full piece; in week two, find images and upload it. [L0679.023]

### Barrier issues

- Older advice (2021-22): Barrier issues are the obstacles that could derail a plan. Write them down next to the Rocks and measurables. [L0679.024]
- When a barrier appears, check whether other measurables or Rocks need to change to free up the resources it needs. For example, if there is not enough time for the content, adjust other commitments to make room. [L0679.024]
- If the owner does not know how to do a required task, that gap is a barrier issue. Solve it by making the acquisition of the missing skill a 90-day Rock, followed by a later Rock for putting that knowledge into practice. [L0679.025]
- Common mistake: do not schedule an entire Rock into a single week. A complex task such as hiring and training someone cannot be done properly within a week. Only a truly urgent item should be squeezed into one week. [L0679.016]

### Rolling 90-day window and quarterly review

- Set Rocks one quarter at a time, always working inside a rolling 90-day window. [L0679.006]
- Each Rock set is reviewed every 90 days, and a new set is defined every quarter. [L0441.003, L0679.003]
- The 90-day window can start at any time and does not have to match the start of a calendar quarter. Standard financial quarters (Q1 to Q4) are optional. [L0679.026]
- The first set of Rocks should cover the starting point toward the one-year plan. Build the 90-day sheet after the three-year picture and the one-year plan are filled in. [L0679.026]

### Linking Rocks to weekly and daily work

- Once the Rocks for the quarter are set, they define the weekly and daily to-do lists. [L0679.006]
