- Worked example of this link: the content Rock is broken into weekly steps, with the full piece written in week one and images found and uploaded in week two. [L0679.023]

### First 90 days: the Source D roadmap

**Overall sequence.** Run the six phases over 90 days in this order:

1. Days 1-14: Foundation [L0199.002]
2. Days 15-30: Product Research & Validation [L0199.002]
3. Days 31-45: Store Building [L0199.002]
4. Days 45-60: Creative Production [L0199.002]
5. Days 60-75: Launching Ads [L0199.002]
6. Days 75-90: Optimization & Scaling [L0199.002]

Note: as the source lists them, day 45 falls in both Phase 3 and Phase 4, and day 60 falls in both Phase 4 and Phase 5. [L0199.002]

**Phase 1 Foundation checklist (Days 1-14).**

1. Understand branded dropshipping as a business model. [L0199.003]
2. Set up Shopify with a custom domain and email address. [L0199.003]
3. Configure Facebook Business Manager and install your Pixel. [L0199.003]
4. Learn the product criteria for dropshipping. [L0199.003]
5. Learn advertising angles and how to identify testable products. [L0199.003]

Spend zero budget on ads in this phase. Focus entirely on building knowledge and technical infrastructure. [L0199.003]

**Rule for Phase 1.** Phase 1 requires zero ad spend. All effort and resources go into technical infrastructure and knowledge, so that Phase 5 ad spending becomes efficient rather than wasteful. [L0199.020]

**Mistake to avoid.** Starting Phase 5 (ad spend) before completing Phases 1-4 wastes budget on untested funnels and unrefined creatives. Each phase builds the knowledge and infrastructure the next one requires, and skipping steps multiplies risk. [L0199.018]

**Expected outcomes after the 90-day roadmap.**

| Area | Outcome |
|---|---|
| Brand | Built from scratch |
| Product | One proven winner identified |
| Creatives | 15-30 distinct assets launched |
| Data capability | Ability to read and interpret ad metrics |
| Profitability | First profitable days or weeks |
| System | A repeatable process for future products |

[L0199.017]

## Plan weeks and days and prioritize

### Calendar as the foundation

- A task or event that is not blocked in the calendar will not happen. Block fixed time for deep work, especially for building web pages or ad creatives, which cannot be done in short gaps [L1181.001].
- Every scheduled task must go into a real calendar or diary with a fixed date, not only into memory. This is a basic part of time management for any business owner [L0384.009].
- Use an electronic calendar such as iCal on Mac or Google Calendar, or any reminder system you already use. Set a fixed time every working day to review the daily to-do list, either early in the morning or in the evening [L0441.005].
- Only schedule daily to-do reviews on days you work on the business. If you work five days a week, prepare Monday's list on Friday afternoon and leave the weekend free. If you work every day, set a fixed time at the end of each week to plan the coming week [L0441.006].
- Schedule planning as recurring calendar blocks: 5 minutes daily for the daily list, 15 minutes weekly for the weekly list, and a longer session each quarter for rocks. Place each block at a time of day that is usually free, so other things cannot take it [L0679.028].
- Work that needs deep focus belongs in blocks of one to two hours. Lighter or ad hoc tasks such as booking calls or catch-up work go into the afternoon [L1181.002].
- Schedule tasks at the time of day when you do your best work. Morning or evening can both work if rest is sufficient [L1181.015].
- Add contingency time to every calendar block, because estimates usually run long. For tasks such as ads, plan considerably more time than expected and leave a buffer between back-to-back calls [L1181.004].
- A rigid weekly schedule tends to break because unplanned events keep appearing. Plan at the level of the day, with time blocks for fixed work and open slots for unpredictable requests [L1181.033].
- Give each task a start and an end time. One speaker estimated that a task could take 20 hours without a time limit but about one hour with a set slot [L0832.007].
- Give each work session a fixed length and a clear output. Without a time cap, tasks such as writing, testing or product research take far longer than needed [L1343.038].
- Use supplier research time, for example while a store is being built, to extract supplier information and fill in a supplier tracking sheet before contacting suppliers [L0934.191].
- Reserve two or three days each week to research new products and check how the market is moving, separate from running existing stores. Use these sessions to build a list of candidate products [L1377.058].

### Weekly planning

- The weekly to-do list must be derived from the rocks. The single most important task needed to reach the current rocks goes at the top of both the weekly and the daily list [L0441.004].
- Treat to-do lists as a habit built with persistence. They act as an anchor that prevents distraction and keeps effort on the right tasks. Without them, progress on important work tends to be lower [L0679.007].
- Weekly planning, in this order (older advice, 2021-22) [L0679.008]:
  1. Once a week, usually on a Friday or a Saturday, block a recurring 15-minute slot in the calendar with phone notifications so it cannot be booked over.
  2. In that slot, review the current 90-day rocks and check what is needed for the coming week.
  3. Write the weekly to-do list for the coming week with no more than 5 items. These are the big items that must be achieved, and they must come from the rocks.
  4. Split each rock into a sequence of tasks. Put only some of them on the weekly list, adding a few more each week, because a complex task such as a hire cannot be completed within one week.
  5. Keep the list and check it against the rocks during the week.
- The weekly list can be written at the end of the previous week or at the start of the new week. Either timing is acceptable, as long as it is written in one of these recurring slots [L0679.030].
- A weekly to-do list must never have more than 5 items [L0679.013].

### Daily planning and the evening routine

- Daily planning, in this order (older advice, 2021-22) [L0679.009]:
  1. At the end of each workday, review how the current day's to-do list went.
  2. Write the to-do list for the next day, with a maximum of 3 must-achieve items, numbered by priority. Number 1 is the highest-value activity.
  3. Place the list where it is always visible, such as on paper next to the computer or on a sticky note on the screen.
  4. The next day, start with item 1 and work through the items in sequence. Do not start non-urgent tasks, such as checking email or social media, until the list is complete.
  5. Create a new list every day.
- Daily to-do lists should be prioritized, giving the day a structured approach to its work [L0223.008].
- Write the to-do list for the next day the night before. The brain settles and starts ordering tasks, so the workday starts without deciding what to do first [L0679.010, L0705.012, L0705.014].
- Evening planning routine [L0705.012]:
  1. Every evening before sleep, write a list of the tasks for the next day.
  2. Use Notion, a piece of paper, a whiteboard or any calendar to organize and manage the tasks.
  3. On waking, review the list so you know exactly what to work on and do not spend the morning deciding.
- A whiteboard can be used to write the next day's to-do list in the evening [L0705.013].
- Place the planning list or whiteboard where it is the first thing you see on waking, so you review the next day's tasks before the morning starts. This removes the time lost to deciding [L0705.016].
- Writing the list in the morning is still better than having no list, but the evening is preferred [L0679.010].
- Plan the day or week the night before. Do not work on the to-do list until the time-critical morning tasks are finished: orders, customer messages, finances and ad checks [L1181.008].
- The daily to-do list is not the whole workday. It holds the must-achieve tasks that drive weekly goals, rocks and yearly targets. Non-urgent activities wait until the first daily item is done, but personal time can be scheduled before or after business work [L0679.017].
- Organize your time with a calendar and a to-do list. Without them the daily workload becomes unmanageable. Block focused work time in the calendar before opening the task list, and do not let open tasks overwhelm the day [L1041.015].
- Calendar and task structure reduce anxiety. Once the day's tasks are known in advance, the mental load of remembering open items drops and focus improves [L1181.016].
- Daily focus routine (Source A) [L1039.014]:
  1. Know your core reason and your dreamline so you know what the work serves.
  2. Plan the day's tasks the night before and work from that list when you sit down.
  3. Focus on one priority task at a time and do not start work without a plan.
  4. Plan tomorrow's tasks before you stop today.

### How many items to plan

| Level | Limit | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Weekly list | No more than 5 items, all from the rocks | [L0679.013, L0679.008] |
| Daily list, older Source B rule | Maximum 3 must-achieve items, numbered by priority | (older, 2021-22) [L0679.013, L0679.009] |
| Daily list, minimum | One must-achieve item is allowed; it still gets the most time and must be finished before non-urgent work. A second or third item is optional | [L0679.031, L0679.013] |
| Daily list, Source C | Aim to finish the top 3; tasks 4 and 5 are a good bonus; tasks 6 to 10 are optional. Not finishing everything is acceptable | [L1181.007] |

- A to-do list of around 15 items is distracting because it is unclear where to start. Only must-achieve items belong on the list, and routine work such as email is never a main driver of growth [L0679.014].
- A rigid list with no open slots fails. Keep some time for unplanned work [L1181.033].

### Prioritizing: the Eisenhower Matrix

- The Eisenhower Matrix sorts all tasks by two dimensions, urgency and importance. Each quadrant gets a different action: immediate execution, strategic scheduling, delegation or deletion [L0265.001].
- Urgency means the task is overdue, has a deadline approaching very soon, demands immediate attention, or would have immediate consequences if not completed [L0265.007].
- Importance means the task affects many people or projects, contributes substantial value to the business, has other tasks dependent on its completion, or offers high reward relative to effort [L0265.006].
- Urgency and importance are independent. Something can be urgent without contributing value, and something can drive large business value without time pressure. Confusing the two makes people work on low-value tasks instead of high-impact ones [L0265.008].

| Quadrant | Urgent | Not urgent |
|---|---|---|
| Important | Execute immediately. Put at the top of the daily, quarterly or annual list [L0265.002] | Schedule intentionally and give consistent daily time. It is the second priority [L0265.003] |
| Not important | Delegate to a virtual assistant or team member [L0265.004] | Remove from the work to-do list entirely [L0265.005] |

- Business owners should spend personal time on important tasks, especially those that are both important and urgent or important but not urgent. Time on urgent but unimportant tasks prevents high-impact work [L0265.017].
- Examples (older advice, 2021-22):
  - Scaling organic traffic from 1,000 to 10,000 monthly visitors is important (a 10x increase typically produces 10x revenue and may improve margins by lowering cost per acquisition) but not urgent. It should sit at the top of the priority list and get daily focus until complete, despite no pressing deadline [L0265.009].
  - Responding to an angry customer is urgent but not important. Delegate it to a customer service virtual assistant [L0265.012].
  - Writing a blog post for SEO growth is important if it serves the organic traffic goal, but not urgent. It should sit high on the list if you handle it personally [L0265.013].
  - Checking email daily is neither important nor urgent in most cases and should never be at the top of the list. Ideally, delegate the email system to a virtual assistant who screens messages and sends a daily summary of items needing your attention [L0265.014].
  - Posting a Black Friday promotion is both urgent and important: high reward with minimal time, potentially thousands of dollars in extra revenue. Execute it immediately [L0265.015].
- Run the tasks you spend the most time on through a prioritization matrix before deciding how much time they deserve (older advice, 2021-22) [L1514.018]:

| | Urgent | Not urgent |
|---|---|---|
| Important | Important and urgent | Important but not urgent |
| Not important | Urgent but not important | Not important and not urgent |

[L1514.018]

### Eat the Frog and task order

- The Frog-Eating Method, attributed to Brian Tracy, says that completing your most unpleasant required task first makes everything else feel easier, raises the chance of finishing the whole list, and builds daily momentum [L0265.010] (older advice, 2021-22 for the Source B source).
- Eat the frog: start with the most difficult or unpleasant task so it is done before the rest of the day [L1181.010].
- Schedule the most important but most difficult or uncomfortable task first thing in the day, using a daily to-do list. Complete it before the less important tasks, which then get smaller time slots in the rest of the day (older advice, 2021-22) [L1514.012].
- Frog-eating procedure (older advice, 2021-22) [L0265.011]:
  1. Sort all tasks into four groups: things you don't want to do but need to do; things you want to do and need to do; things you want to do but don't need to do; things you don't want to do and don't need to do.
  2. Complete group 1 first. These often involve difficult conversations or financial discomfort.
  3. Complete group 2 second.
  4. Complete group 3 last.
  5. Outsource or delete group 4 entirely.
- Conflicting view: Source A (2025) found that starting the workday with the simplest task builds momentum, and that moving to harder tasks once in work mode works better for him. Starting with the hardest task can also work, but it often delays the start [L0972.092]. The same source notes that the classic Eat That Frog advice is to do the most avoided task first, and that starting with an easy task worked better for daily momentum [L0972.093].
- Which to use: Use the frog method (hardest task first) when the hard task is the one that blocks the day and you can start it right away. Use the simple-first method (warm-up, then harder work) when starting the hard task tends to delay the start. Both sources agree that the daily list must be set in advance [L0972.092, L0972.093, L0265.010, L1181.010].

### Staying on the plan during the day

- Once daily priorities are set with the Eisenhower Matrix or the frog method, do not second-guess or change them mid-day. Working the list without deviation increases the sense of progress and accomplishment (older advice, 2021-22) [L0265.018].
- Focus on one task at a time. Holding several unfinished jobs in mind creates tension and lowers the quality of each one [L1206.019].
- Plan the day around the top priorities, not the whole backlog [L1181.007].
- Work does not need to be completed in full every day. Missing some items is acceptable if the top priorities are done [L1181.007].

### Handling distracting ideas

- Distractions that pop up during a task, such as a new post idea, should not interrupt the current task. Write them on the back of the daily list to record them without breaking focus (older advice, 2021-22) [L0679.018].
- Keep the phone away during work time to protect focus. Use paper and pen for daily task lists instead of digital tools (older advice, 2021-22) [L0223.007].
- Handling distracting ideas, in this order (older advice, 2021-22) [L0679.020]:
  1. When a new idea comes up during a task, write it on the back of the daily to-do sheet and keep working on the current task.
  2. At the end of the day, when writing the next day's list, review everything written on the back of the sheet.
  3. For each idea, decide whether to add it to the next day's list (if important enough), keep it as a record for later, or turn it into a future rock.
- Ideas are not a substitute for execution. Staying in the visionary role only, with many ideas and no execution, produces no results. Schedule fixed execution time so the idea phase never takes over the whole day [L0519.007].
- Many entrepreneurs have more ideas than time, so the real skill is choosing what to work on. Focus on the highest-impact task for the current stage of the store (2018, older) [L1274.080].
- Everyone has the same number of hours per day, so the question is where to invest them. Time on compounding work matters more than filler time that feels productive (2019, older) [L1275.059].

### Decision limits and the value of time

- A solo owner can make only a limited number of high-quality decisions per day and per week. Quality drops once the limit is exceeded. Lack of focus is a major barrier for small and medium-sized businesses, which will not reach their potential if the owner is spread across too many opportunities [L0679.029].
- Each person can make only a limited number of high-quality decisions per day or week. Planning the to-do list in advance saves decision capacity for business decisions that come up during the day. Routines such as a fixed breakfast or similar clothing serve the same purpose (older advice, 2021-22) [L0679.011].
- For a small to medium e-commerce business run by one person or a small team, the owner's ability to perform, manage time, focus and set priorities largely determines how fast it grows (older advice, 2021-22) [L0802.001].
- Start each new calendar year with a dedicated block of owner-focused preparation: time management, focus, prioritization and delegation. Do this before executing the year's marketing plans. The block needs no marketing tactics (older advice, 2021-22) [L0802.011].
- Judge the value of current time by the business you expect in about two years, not by the hourly income you earn today. Intense early phases often pay off later, like a snowball effect [L1359.012].
- Organization is necessary for growth. Missed deadlines, calls and commitments have real consequences, and structured time management is essential when one person handles many roles [L1181.025].
- Avoid over-engineering the task system. Keep it simple, and add complexity such as custom fields or extra views only when a specific process needs it [L1181.017].

### Tools and task systems

- Paper is the preferred tool for weekly and daily lists because it stays visible and feels real. A single sheet is enough without fancy templates (older advice, 2021-22) [L0679.012].
- Weekly and daily to-do lists do not need software. They can be written by hand. A workable routine is to write the daily list the night before, so the next day starts with the top priority already set (older advice, 2021-22) [L0441.014].
- Notion, paper, a whiteboard or any calendar can organize and manage daily tasks [L0705.013].
- Notion can hold calendars, to-do lists, projects and sprints in one place, which makes it the preferred organization tool for running a dropshipping business. The operator moved from Slack to Trello before settling on Notion [L0519.008].
- Deep Work by Cal Newport helps build a focused work environment, which is especially useful for the fabric-worker role. Getting Things Done by David Allen helps keep tasks from piling up as stress [L0519.010].

### Three-hat method

- The three-hat method splits work into three roles. The fabric worker does the tasks. The manager plans, delegates and checks the work. The visionary generates new ideas and products. Each role needs its own fixed time block so that ideas never crowd out execution [L0519.005].
- Build a color-coded daily schedule for the three roles. Example from the lesson: fabric worker black, manager green, visionary beige (Source C) [L0519.006]:
  1. Morning: read for about one hour to collect ideas and inspiration.
  2. From 10 a.m.: work in 25-minute shifts, taking one task at a time and finishing it.
  3. Afternoon: fixed slot for client or student calls.
  4. After the calls: manager hat. Check the morning's finished tasks and prepare the next day's task list.
  5. Then use the time for skill learning, for example language lessons.
  6. Stop work, exercise, and start the next day on the same schedule.
  7. Evening: keep a slot for free thinking, because new ideas often come during other activities.

## Audit your time and remove distractions

### Measuring where your time goes

- Before any optimization, the owner needs a baseline: a detailed record of where time actually goes, not a general sense of what was done. Without this measured starting point, later changes cannot be judged. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.001]
- Time is the one resource an e-commerce owner cannot increase. The day has a fixed 24 hours, and customers only shop for limited hours. The only lever is using existing hours better: choosing what to work on, setting priorities, and reducing procrastination. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.002]
- Owners tend to drift into short-term tasks that feel productive but do not drive medium- to long-term growth. Concentrate on the tasks only you can do, and stay focused while doing them. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.003]
- The more focused the owner is on the business, the greater the chance it grows and succeeds. Distractions reduce that chance directly, so protecting focus time is part of the plan. [L1043.010]
- Constant urgency is often perception rather than reality. Protecting focus time is more valuable than responding to every request immediately, since attention is one of the most valuable resources an entrepreneur has. [L1181.006]
- Do not blame yourself for struggling with time management. Most business owners find it hard. Gradual, consistent improvement in how time is used reduces stress and pressure and improves confidence in the business. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.017]

### Running the 30-day baseline

**Procedure (about four calendar weeks):**

1. For the next four weeks, record every task performed in or on the business. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.004]
2. Record the estimated time spent on each task. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.004]
3. Break the record down day by day. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.004]
4. Include every distraction, for example a 30-minute detour into Facebook during a work block. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.004]
5. Add a procrastination line each day with the total estimated time lost to procrastination. Details of what was done are optional. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.004]
6. Enter the entries at the end of each day, as you go, or as a running log. Choose what works, but the entries must accurately reflect what was actually done. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.004]
7. Review the 30 days afterward to identify patterns and decide what to change. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.004]

**Steps that make the baseline work:**

- Set a daily reminder in the phone or calendar so that the time log is filled in every day for the full 30 days. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.012]
- Start the time log immediately, on the day you first set it up, instead of postponing it until other planning or learning is finished. Setup takes only a couple of minutes, so there is no reason to delay the 30-day baseline. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.014]
- Tracking does not have to run continuously. It can be done periodically, and a one-month tracking run is enough to refresh your view of how time is being spent. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.019]

### Tools for time tracking

- **Toggl Track** (toggl.com/track) is an app and online service that automatically times activities on a computer and, optionally, on a phone. You do not have to remember to start and stop each task. It can show how much time goes to apps such as Instagram. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.008] The review unit names it "Toggle." [L1514.001]
- **Manual day-by-day spreadsheet tracker**, available in Excel and Numbers versions. Each day is a column. Each row is a task with the time spent, and a procrastination row is included. Add a column for each further day to extend the tracker beyond the template's sample days. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.009]

**Example entries for one Monday:**

| Task | Time |
|---|---|
| Customer service phone calls | 1.5 hours |
| Writing product descriptions for the website | 1 hour |
| Updating business social media pages | 30 minutes |

(older, 2021-22) [L0442.010]

### Logging honestly

- Time tracking only works if the owner is honest with themselves. Recording distractions and procrastination can feel uncomfortable, but skipping them makes the baseline useless. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.005]
- The tracking records are private and are not shared with a coach or anyone else. Their purpose is to reveal patterns in your own working time. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.006]
- Do not analyze or change working habits during the 30 days of tracking. First collect the data. Patterns and corrective actions come after the tracking period ends. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.007]
- Totaling the day at the end relies on memory, which makes accurate tracking harder for people who struggle to recall their day. Logging each task as it happens is more reliable. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.011]
- A time log only shows the real picture if it includes distractions and detours, not just planned work blocks. Record examples such as:
  - switching to another website or social media during a work block
  - checking the phone instead of working
  - deciding to leave for a break, such as an hour away

  Log each one with its estimated duration. (older, 2021-22) [L0442.013]

### Reviewing the log after 30 days

**Procedure:** Review the time-tracking log after at least 30 days (a practical minimum, a month of tracking). Find four categories: distractions, rapid task switching, long stretches on unimportant tasks, and tasks that could be delegated. Use an app such as Toggl Track or a spreadsheet to pull the data. Then check each category in order: (older, 2021-22) [L1514.001]

1. **Distractions:** note interruptions in the middle of a task and off-topic breaks, and count how often each one recurs. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.001]
2. **Task switching:** look for many short blocks of 15 minutes or less on the same kind of work. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.001]
3. **Unimportant tasks:** find activities that take long stretches of time, and classify them with a prioritization matrix (an important/urgent grid). (older, 2021-22) [L1514.001]
4. **Delegation:** find tasks taking more than one hour per day, especially recurring ones over 30 days, and ask whether only the owner can do them. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.001]

**Deciding what counts as a priority:**

- Treat a distraction as a priority problem only when it recurs often over the tracking period. A distraction seen once in 30 days may not matter. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.002]
- Classify each recurring distraction in two ways: as business-related or not, and as something you control or not. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.002]
- The worst distractions are the ones that keep appearing at the same point in the daily schedule. A single occurrence in a 30-day tracking period is usually not worth acting on. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.020]

### Distraction types and how to handle them

**Habit-based distractions**

- Distractions that come from habits are addictive behaviors that persist until you deliberately break the habit with a specific plan. Examples include checking email, social media, or the Shopify app repeatedly, such as 57 times a day. Such habits do not speed up sales or business growth. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.003]

**Business-related distractions: scheduling**

1. Decide whether the distraction can be moved to a later time. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.004]
2. Add it to the daily planner at a fixed time instead of dropping it entirely. For example, 30 minutes of email at 1 p.m. after important work is finished. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.004]
3. Enforce the schedule through self-discipline, since no tool can enforce it for you. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.004]

**Uncontrollable interruptions**

- Interruptions you cannot control need a different solution than habit-based distractions. Examples include a business phone ringing before a customer service agent exists, or a family member entering unexpectedly. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.005]
- Address the phone-ringing case through staffing or outsourcing rather than willpower. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.005]

### Task switching

- Each interruption to a task costs about 15 minutes to return to the flow state, so every switch loses at least 15 minutes of focused work. (older, 2021-22) [L0679.019]
- Task switching is inefficient when you work on the same task in blocks of 15 minutes or less. The brain needs at least 10 minutes to reach peak efficiency on a task, so short repeated blocks lose a large share of productive time. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.006]
- **Example:** A blog post written in four 20-minute blocks on different days took 80 minutes of time across the sessions. When the same post was written in one 80-minute block, it took about 60 minutes, because focus was sustained and work quality rose. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.007]
- **Example, same case within one day:** A blog post written in four separate 20-minute blocks within one day took 80 minutes in total. When the same post was written in one continuous 80-minute block, it took about 60 minutes, because the writer kept full focus and the quality was higher. Combining the fragments into one block of the same task saves time. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.021]
- Combine short fragments of the same task into one longer block, around 60 to 80 minutes, to finish faster. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.007]

**Procedure to reduce task switching:**

1. Identify tasks you switch between often in blocks of 15 minutes or less. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.008]
2. If you are not using a daily to-do list, start one and build a habit around it. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.008]
3. If you already have one, enforce it with self-discipline and reward yourself for sticking to it. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.008]
4. Schedule the task in one larger block. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.008]

### Comfort tasks and ad account over-checking

- Spending more than one hour per day on tasks that are unimportant for the business is a warning sign. Review these tasks with a prioritization matrix. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.009]
- People often spend too long on unimportant tasks they feel comfortable with, such as hours in the Google Ads account. They avoid important tasks they feel less confident about, such as SEO or difficult supplier or customer conversations. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.010]
- For a high-ticket dropshipping business, SEO has far greater potential than Google Ads. Spending hours on ads while neglecting SEO means the time is misallocated. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.010]
- Optimizing Google Ads has diminishing returns, and you can easily spend hours on it. Spending five hours a week to raise CTR by 0.3% rarely produces revenue or profit worth that time, and those hours could go to other work. (older, 2021-22) [L0879.036]

**Ad account time limits:**

- Ads account work needs hours daily only in rare cases. Setting up a new account, building new campaigns, or making major changes justifies extra time. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.011]
- Routine data review and small tweaks that take hours are over-analysis. Limit them, for example to 30 minutes instead of two hours. (older, 2021-22) [L1514.011]

**Over-optimizing by hand:**

- Refusing automation and optimizing every bid by hand in spreadsheets takes very many hours. One keyword example was estimated at 20 hours. That time is then missing for conversion rate optimization, store branding, and other growth work. [L0454.012]

### Protecting focus blocks

**Blocked work sessions:**

1. Use Do Not Disturb or focus mode during a blocked work session. [L1181.005]
2. Silence phone notifications and close apps. [L1181.005]
3. Handle urgent calls or messages only if they are repeated or clearly an emergency. [L1181.005]

**Unfocused time:**

- Two hours of checking email, searching, and messaging without a defined task does not count as productive work. Recognize unfocused time as lost time, not as progress. [L1039.015]

**Supplier outreach:**

- A common failure point is avoiding supplier outreach, especially phone calls. Postponing calls to a later weekend or vacation stops progress. Schedule the calls and make them. [L0934.110, L0934.187] (Source A, 2023)

## Find and break one weakness with discipline and habits

### Why repeated action beats revenue targets

- Focus the business strategy on meaningful repeated actions rather than revenue targets, because small actions compound into much greater results. [L0107.001]
- Small meaningful actions repeated consistently produce inevitable growth, and the absence of repeated meaningful action produces inevitable decline. [L0107.002]
- Older (2021-22): six months of consistent daily practice in a skill produces noticeable improvement and opens opportunities you would not have pursued at the start. [L0107.003]

### Identifying business and personal weaknesses

- Weaknesses change as the business moves to a new revenue level. A weakness that mattered at startup is often not the one that matters at a multi-million-dollar stage, so reassess weaknesses whenever the business reaches a new stage. [L0443.001]
- Older (2021-22): a business doing around 2 to 3 million USD in annual revenue has a different set of weak points than a startup. Use revenue level as the trigger to re-audit weaknesses. [L0443.009]
- A weakness is also a hidden opportunity. Once you identify a weak point, fixing it gives you a concrete way to grow the business. [L0443.002]
- Every weakness contains an opportunity. For example, a business with no organic traffic that depends entirely on paid ads has the opportunity to build an SEO strategy. [L0581.007]
- Weaknesses come from two sources, and both must be checked when auditing:
  - **The business:** products sold, suppliers used, marketing and advertising approach.
  - **The owner:** not committing enough time, lacking focus. [L0443.003]
- Owners tend to spend most of their time where they feel strong and confident, which leaves weaker areas neglected. Recognise this comfort-zone trap when reviewing how time is spent. [L0443.004]
- Operational weak spots, such as manufacturing or admin, can be built step by step. Aim to move from a low self-rated skill level toward a solid one over time. [L1225.081]
- To identify a weakness, ask yourself why you are not yet where you want to be in your business or in life. Write the answers separately for business weaknesses and personal weaknesses, since the two often overlap. [L0581.031]

### Documenting the weakness in your review

- Identify and document at least one business weakness during your business review, so your improvement effort has a focus for the coming period. [L0223.005]

**Procedure: choosing the weakness from the annual review** [L0443.008]

1. Open the annual review you wrote for the previous year.
2. Identify your biggest business weakness from the list, usually the item at the top.
3. Write that weakness down as a single note.
4. Set the goal of fixing this weakness over the next six months.
5. Plan the concrete steps in the following work sessions.

### Choosing one weakness for six months

- Pick only one biggest business weakness as the target for the next six months instead of working through the whole list at once. Write it down as a single note before planning the steps to fix it. [L0443.010]
- Work on one weakness at a time. Spreading effort across several changes at once lowers the chance that any of them actually changes, although the chosen weakness can be a big one. [L0581.001]
- Plan a six-month focus on breaking one chosen weakness. Only one weakness is targeted in that period, and the work continues until it is no longer one of your significant weaknesses. [L0581.006]
- Put in place specific steps and habits designed to break each identified weakness, and begin practising them immediately rather than waiting for a future date. [L0223.006]
- At the six-month mark (mid-year review), identify a new business weakness for the next six months. Rotate the improvement focus from one area to another. [L0223.010]

### Why weaknesses stay unfixed

- Most people fail to fix a weakness for three reasons: they never identify it clearly, they try to do too much too soon, and they lack the discipline and consistency to let a change run its course. Identifying a business or personal weakness in writing already puts you ahead of most people. [L0581.002]
- Unrealistic goals, such as trying to fix everything at once, usually lead to failure. A focused and realistic goal for a single weakness is the better approach. [L0581.003]
- Many people abandon a change after a week or two because it does not show results fast enough. A new practice or habit needs time before it can take effect. [L0581.004]

### Self-discipline

- Breaking a weakness rests on three elements: self-discipline, consistency and habits. Each one must be developed deliberately. [L0581.008]
- Self-discipline is a positive, self-driven decision to commit to a goal and follow the course needed to reach it. No one else can impose it, and no external punishment or accountability is involved. [L0581.009]
- Successful business owners differ from unsuccessful ones mainly in self-discipline and consistency, not in secret knowledge or unique ideas. Without the ability to stay consistent on necessary work, the chance of success drops significantly. [L0581.005]
- Older (2020): the book *Do the Work* by Steven Pressfield is a short read recommended for beginners who struggle with motivation and need to stay focused on consistent action. [L0852.013]

**Procedure: commitment statement for self-discipline** [L0581.010]

1. Write on a piece of paper the statement "I will break my [weakness]", naming the specific weakness.
2. Place the paper where you will see it every day, such as above your computer, on your mirror, on your fridge, or as your phone or desktop background.
3. Read the statement out loud every day for about six months, or until the weakness is no longer one of your significant weaknesses in the business.

### Consistency parameters

- Consistency means following the chosen course in the same way every day until the goal is reached or the weakness is broken. Easy-to-achieve daily parameters for time, finances and output make this possible, because overly large commitments lead to discouragement. [L0581.011]
- Time-based parameter, email marketing: 15 minutes a day on email work, such as copy, list segmentation or automations, every day you work on the business. Committing to writing emails for eight hours a day for a month is not achievable for most people, who find long writing sessions mentally daunting. [L0581.012]
- Output-based parameter: set a fixed daily result instead of a time limit, such as writing one new email per day. The time this takes varies with the length of the email, but the daily output stays the same. [L0581.013]
- Recurring SEO task: 30 minutes a day is an achievable and sustainable parameter, whereas committing to SEO work for eight hours a day indefinitely is not. [L0581.015]

**Procedure: setting a consistency action** [L0581.014]

1. Identify one task or behaviour that will break the weakness, or one thing you will consistently not do if the weakness is a bad habit.
2. Define an achievable parameter for it in time, finances or output.
3. Write the task and its parameter down.

### Habit loop: trigger, action, reward

A habit is formed by performing three steps consistently for about three to four weeks. For some habits it takes longer. Keep repeating the steps on every relevant day until the action happens without conscious thought. [L0581.016]

| Loop element | What it is | Example | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Trigger | Environmental cue that it is time to act | Recurring calendar entry plus hard-to-ignore notifications on computer, phone and smartwatch | [L0581.018, L0581.019] |
| Action | Start immediately when the trigger appears | Switch to a 30-minute task the moment the trigger goes off | [L0581.020] |
| Reward | Small, pleasurable, given after every completion | Dark chocolate, a favourite TV series, an outdoor activity, or half an hour or an hour of games after two hours of business work | [L0581.021, L0581.023] |

- Every habit, good or bad, has a trigger, which is an environmental cue signalling that it is time to act. Removing or changing the cue behind a bad habit is an effective way to break it. [L0581.018]
- A trigger for a daily one-hour writing block can be a recurring calendar entry saying it is time to start, combined with notifications that are hard to ignore on the computer, the phone and a smartwatch alarm. [L0581.019]
- Once the trigger goes off, start the action straight away, for example by switching to a 30-minute task the moment the trigger appears. During the three to four week building phase this depends on self-discipline. Planning your days and weeks around triggers helps you respond when other commitments come up. [L0581.020]
- Give a reward immediately after each completed action, every time, because the reward reinforces the habit. The reward should be small and pleasurable and does not need to be large. [L0581.021]
- Examples of rewards include a piece of dark chocolate after a task, or a favourite TV series, an enjoyable outdoor activity, or video games for a set period after a block of work, such as half an hour or an hour of games after two hours of business work. [L0581.023]
- Habits that give the brain a dopamine reward are very hard to break, because the brain subconsciously craves that outcome. Replacing such a reward with a different, enjoyable one is more realistic than simply removing it. [L0581.022]
- A habit only needs to be performed on the days it is relevant, for example on the days you work on the business. If you do not work on weekends, the habit does not apply on weekends. [L0581.024]
- Habits are formed and broken more easily when practised in a relaxed state, with a clear picture of the desired end result in mind. Trying hard to resist a habit often reinforces it. [L1206.029]
- Habit change works through both avoiding a habit and deliberately performing a new behaviour, as long as the desired end result stays clearly in mind. Straining against a habit with willpower tends to reinforce it. Practising the new behaviour calmly and without effort is more effective. [L1206.039]

**Procedure: setting up the weakness-breaking habit** [L0581.029]

1. Identify one beneficial habit that reflects the chosen weakness, and the specific task you must do consistently to break it.
2. Set an environmental trigger for that habit.
3. Set a small, pleasurable reward for completing it.
4. Take the action every time the trigger occurs, straight away, and collect the reward right after each completion. Put all of this in place and start the next day.

**Procedure: building a new habit** (repeat on every relevant day for about three to four weeks) [L0581.017]

1. Set a trigger, which is an environmental cue telling you it is time to take the action.
2. When the trigger appears, start the action immediately, with no delay.
3. Give yourself a reward right after finishing the action, every single time.

### Breaking bad habits

- Breaking a bad habit is harder than building a new one. [L0581.025]

**Procedure: breaking a bad habit** [L0581.025]

1. Remove or replace the trigger, for example by avoiding the people, places or situations that set off the habit.
2. Make the action harder to perform, for example by never keeping the item at home or in the fridge.
3. Change or replace the reward with a healthier or more beneficial one.

### Starting small and growing the habit

- Making a habit too big or complex, or building several habits at the same time, is a common reason habits fail. An unachievable habit, such as eight hours of SEO work a day, will not be maintained for the three to four weeks needed to form it. [L0581.026]

**Procedure: growing a new habit gradually** [L0581.027]

1. Start with a very small version of the action, such as 5 to 10 minutes of copywriting practice per day.
2. Once that is consistent, increase the time by a modest percentage, for example from 10 minutes to 20 minutes per day.
3. Keep building on each new level, so the small steps compound into a much bigger habit over time.

### Compounding small gains

- Aim to be about 1% better each day than your own version of the day before. Small consistent gains add up to visible results over time. [L0631.005]
- Older (2021-22): a daily improvement of 1 percent in a business compounds to about 37 percent growth over one year. Small consistent gains therefore add up significantly. [L0581.028]
- Daily compounding check: 1.01 raised to the 365th power is about 37.8, so a 1 percent daily improvement sustained for a full year produces roughly 37 to 38 percent growth. The gain comes from the daily repetition, not from any single large change. [L0581.030]

## Set up accountability and tracking systems

### Start-up sequence for community and tracking

Start-up sequence, community and tracking, in order:

1. Join the Discord community. [L0393.001, L0532.001]
2. Create a progress thread on Discord to log your work as you go. [L0393.001, L0532.001]

### Daily accountability form

- Complete the form at the close of every day. The form is a mandatory part of the coaching program, so it must be submitted at the end of every single day with no exceptions. [L0507.004]
- The form is drafted in a Google Doc. Copy the finished form into your personal ticket in the program's Discord server, which is where the daily submission is made. [L0507.003]
- The Google Doc saves to Google Drive automatically while you edit it, so the status shows "Saved to Drive" after changes. [L0507.012]
- The daily progress check asks whether 100% of the day's planned tasks were completed. Anything below 100% requires a written explanation of what caused the shortfall. [L0507.005]
- Common reasons to log when tasks are not completed include distractions, poor time management, unclear priorities, and having to attend an appointment. [L0507.007]
- Productivity is self-rated daily on a scale from 1 (unproductive) to 10 (fully locked in). [L0507.006]
- Naming the specific obstacle that stopped you is a form of self-accountability. Acknowledging what happened, rather than hiding it, is what makes the daily review useful. [L0507.008]
- Daily self-reporting shows a coach where progress is stalling and how to help. The person must still take responsibility for their own results, because outside support has limits. [L0507.009]
- Skipping the daily form or leaving the "what stopped you" answer vague undermines the review, since the reasons for missed tasks are the information needed to fix them. [L0507.010]

Procedure for completing the form:

1. Open the daily accountability form document (Google Docs) and copy it. [L0507.001]
2. Open your ticket in the Discord server and paste the form into it. [L0507.001]
3. Enter today's date at the top of the form. [L0507.001]
4. Enter your name. [L0507.001]
5. Answer whether you completed 100% of today's tasks, with Yes or No. [L0507.001]
6. If you answered No, write what stopped you from completing everything. [L0507.001]
7. Fill in Tomorrow's Game Plan: your top 3 priorities for tomorrow and any other tasks. [L0507.001]
8. Fill in the Daily Reflection: a summary of today, what went well, what did not, and what you will improve tomorrow. [L0507.001]
9. Give a Productivity Rating from 1 (unproductive) to 10 (fully locked in). [L0507.001]
10. Write the Final Reflection and submit the form. [L0507.001]

Order of writing at the end of the day: list the top 3 priorities for tomorrow plus other tasks, then summarize today, note what went well, what did not, and what to improve tomorrow, before giving the productivity rating. [L0507.011]

Template text:

```
DAILY ACCOUNTABILITY FORM

Complete this form at the close of every day.

Date: [write today's date, e.g. 22nd October]
Name: [your name]

1. Daily Progress Check
Were all of today's tasks finished, 100%? (Yes / No)
If not, what got in the way? Answer honestly and with specifics. (For example: distractions, poor time management, unclear priorities, an appointment, etc.)

2. Tomorrow's Game Plan
Top 3 Priorities for Tomorrow:
1.
2.
3.
Other Tasks:

3. Daily Reflection
Summary of Today:
What went well?
What didn't?
What will you improve tomorrow?

4. Productivity Rating (1 = unproductive, 10 = fully locked in): [1-10]

5. Final Reflection: [write your closing thoughts for the day]
```
[L0507.002]

- In the Date field, write the day and month in short numeric form, for example 22/10, rather than only a spoken date. Enter the name exactly as it should appear for the coach to recognize you. [L0507.013]

End-of-day routine with metrics:

1. Open the daily accountability form template and choose File > Make a copy. [L1323.008]
2. Fill in the date and your name. [L1323.008]
3. Answer whether you completed 100% of your planned tasks, and if not, what stopped you. [L1323.008]
4. Write tomorrow's game plan with your top 3 priorities. [L1323.008]
5. Write a daily reflection: a summary of the day, what went well, what did not, and what you will improve tomorrow. [L1323.008]
6. Record today's metrics: amount spent, purchases, add to carts, cost per acquisition and ROAS. [L1323.008]
7. Send the completed form to your coach or support channel before the day ends. [L1323.008]

### Daily habit tracker in Google Sheets

- A daily tracker holds you accountable for each day's planned actions. Writing down every day's plan and checking it off builds the habits needed for larger income goals. [L0456.001]
- Schedule each recurring habit as a fixed 30-minute block. Spend at least 30 minutes per day on each habit, and extend it to double, triple or quadruple time when possible. [L0456.002]
- Market research is tracked as a daily 30-minute habit. Three 30-minute habit blocks in one day add up to 90 minutes of planned daily work. [L0456.003]
- Three separate 30-minute blocks add up to 90 minutes of daily tasks. Each extra 30-minute block raises the planned daily total by 30 minutes. [L0456.004]
- A 30 Mins Development block can be filled with training, a course, a YouTube video or a book. Studying a losing ad and writing down why it failed and how to improve it also counts toward this habit. [L0456.014]
- The daily 30 Mins Development row is left blank on Sundays, so that habit runs on six days per week by default. [L0456.017]

Template layout:

| Element | Specification | IDs |
|---|---|---|
| Tabs | One tab per quarter: Q1 to Q4 | L0456.005 |
| Blocks | One block per month within each tab | L0456.005 |
| Rows | One row per habit | L0456.005 |
| Daily marks | TRUE/FALSE per day in each habit row | L0456.005 |
| Columns | Total, Goal, Complete, Reward | L0456.005 |
| Day headers | M, T, W, T, F, S, S per week | L0456.005 |

- Weekly habits such as New Idea To Growth Guide, Launch New Ad Creative and Attend Weekly Call are marked only once every seven days, always on the same weekday. All other days in those habit rows stay empty. [L0456.016]
- In the October block of the Q4 tab, the template replaces New Idea To Growth Guide and Launch New Ad Creative with Market Research and Go To The Gym. The November and December blocks in the same tab return to the standard set of habits. [L0456.018]

Daily tracking routine:

1. Make a copy of the habit tracker template (File > Make a copy) so you own your version. [L0456.012]
2. Schedule each habit as a 30-minute block in your day, at least 30 minutes per day for each habit. [L0456.012]
3. Each day, set the habit cell to TRUE if it was done and FALSE if it was not. [L0456.012]
4. For every missed habit, right-click the cell and add a note stating why it was not done. [L0456.012]
5. Check the Complete value for each habit and aim for at least 75%. [L0456.012]
6. Once a habit reaches 75% completion, give yourself a reward. [L0456.012]

- For a missed habit, right-click the cell in the Google Sheet and choose to add a note. Write the specific reason the task was not done, for example why the gym session was skipped. [L0456.009]
- Skipping a habit without documenting the reason removes the learning from the tracker. Vague excuses such as feeling sick are not useful; record the concrete cause in the cell note. [L0456.010]

### Weekly and monthly goals

- Post weekly goals in the community and follow up on them a week later, so that you are held accountable. (Older advice, 2021.) [L0671.147, L0671.281]
- Goal accountability routine (2025):
  1. Collect each person's annual goal in writing. [L0807.002]
  2. Record name, community username and goal in one tracking spreadsheet. [L0807.002]
  3. Review each goal at every monthly check-in, noting what was tried, what changed and what to try next. [L0807.002]
- Set one measurable goal per month and have it recorded in a shared tracking sheet maintained by the group lead. Report progress at each check-in to keep yourself accountable. [L0857.070]
- Set clear monthly goals and report progress regularly in a shared tracker, so that execution stays on pace and goals are reviewed. [L0966.104]
- Set a monthly goal and update your progress each month. Tracking goals in a shared progress sheet keeps you accountable. [L0972.091]
- Trello (free) can track goals with checklists and track content with lists, for example Ideas, Drafts and Published, with each post as a card moved between lists. [L0808.050]

### Weekly review and changelog

Keep the changelog up to date with a weekly routine:

1. Set a recurring reminder on your phone, for example every Monday or every Friday. [L0410.010]
2. At each reminder, open the changelog and check it. [L0410.010]
3. Add any changes made since the last check. [L0410.010]
4. Enter the results of any experiments whose result date has passed. [L0410.010]
5. Review past experiments before proposing a new idea, to avoid retesting something that already failed. [L0410.010]

### Whiteboard with checkboxes

- A whiteboard placed where you see it every day works as an accountability tool. It shows your goals and the tasks you must do, and each item has a box you tick once it is done. It works like a daily tracker but keeps your actions in front of you more constantly. [L0870.001]
- Writing goals and tasks down in a visible place outside your head makes them clearer and easier to follow through on consistently. [L0870.007]
- Board procedure:
  1. Write your goals on the whiteboard. [L0870.002]
  2. Draw a small empty box next to each goal. [L0870.002]
  3. Below the goals, write the tasks you need to do, each with its own box. [L0870.002]
  4. Look at the board every day and tick a box only if that goal or task was actually achieved. [L0870.002]
- Example: a daily goal written as a 10k day, with a box beside it that you tick yes or no at the end of the day depending on whether you reached that amount. [L0870.003]
- Write realistic, small goals that you can actually achieve within the time frame, not unrealistic dreams such as earning one million dollars. Goals you can check off are the ones that keep the board useful. [L0870.004]
- Small wins accumulate over time into large results, so consistent small achievements matter more than waiting for one big leap. [L0870.005]
- A visible unchecked box creates a feeling of guilt each time you see it, and this discomfort pushes you to act on the open task. The board works because the open box stays in front of you every day until you tick it. [L0870.006]
- Put on the board the tasks you are avoiding, not only the easy ones. A difficult task that stays unticked keeps pressing on you each day, which is the pressure the board is meant to create. [L0870.012]

Buying and placement:

- A magnetic whiteboard listed on Shopee came with 3 markers and 1 eraser, in sizes 30 x 45 cm, 45 x 60 cm and 60 x 90 cm, at a price range of $16.80 to $39.90 with free shipping. [L0870.008]
- Buy the whiteboard from an online marketplace such as Shopee or Lazada, or from the nearest store that sells stationery. The budget should not exceed $40. [L0870.009]
- On the Shopee listing shown, the seller offered 15-day returns with no questions asked and a Damage Protection plan for 6 months from delivery. The plan covered total loss from accidental and liquid damage as well as wear and tear. [L0870.010]
- A magnetic whiteboard sold on Shopee was described as lightweight and suitable for wall-mounting. A wall-hung board can sit where you see it daily instead of being stored away. [L0870.013]
- Place the whiteboard where you will see it first thing every morning, such as a wall you pass when you wake up. Daily visibility is what keeps unticked goals and tasks in front of you until they are done. [L0870.011]

### Diary of actions and logbook

- Track daily business actions in a diary (for example, "completed product research today") rather than measuring success only by money. Daily habits compound into long-term success. [L0280.039]
- A logbook is a simple daily record of what you worked on, where you went and what you did. It does not need to be a detailed diary. Over several years, these short entries help you recall details and show how far your work has progressed. [L1343.012]
- Wall calendar procedure:
  1. Buy a wall calendar that shows the whole year. [L1343.011]
  2. Break your work into daily tasks that take a set amount of time. [L1343.011]
  3. When each day's task is finished, mark a big X in that day's box. [L1343.011]
  4. Aim only to fill each box and never break the chain of marked days. [L1343.011]
- Logbook procedure:
  1. Keep a short daily logbook of what you worked on, where you went and what you did. [L1343.043]
  2. Each evening, write down the best thing that happened that day, rather than only the problems. [L1343.043]
  3. Review the entries after a few weeks or months to spot patterns in your work and energy. [L1343.043]
- Finishing assigned tasks before the next follow-up call, without needing reminders, was linked to the fastest progress in a mentoring setting. Being accountable and self-directed moves a beginner forward more than advice alone. [L0819.032]

### Progress threads and public accountability

To use Progress Threads:

1. Open the "Progress Threads" section. [L0517.007]
2. Start your own thread. [L0517.007]
3. Introduce yourself, state your starting point and your goals. [L0517.007]
4. Update the thread as you move through the program so others can follow your progress. [L0517.007]

- Progress Threads create accountability. Publicly posting updates on goals and progress keeps members committed, and following other members' threads gives everyone a way to track each other's journey. [L0517.008]

### Milestones and celebration

- Celebrate small milestones along the way, such as the first supplier approval, the first recovered abandoned cart, or the first sale. Each one signals that the next level has been reached and makes the following step easier. [L0826.007]
- Celebrate milestones as they happen, including the first sale, the first profitable store, the first 10,000-dollar month and a 10,000-dollar day such as Cyber Monday. Celebration keeps the team motivated during slow periods. [L0832.021]
- After a first sale with about 100 dollars of profit, one operator spent about 500 dollars on a celebration and the whole profit was gone within seconds. Set a celebration budget that stays below the profit actually earned in that period. [L0832.056]

### Milestone rewards and leaderboards

- Milestone rewards can motivate students, for example a one-on-one call unlocked at 5k in sales or a bonus at 10k in sales. A community leaderboard that ranks members by engagement can add further motivation. [L1017.009]

### Gamifying results and rewards

- Gamifying your business means attaching rewards to measurable numbers, so each win reinforces continued effort in the same way reward loops work in video games. Any business or life result that can be counted can be gamified. [L1039.025]
- Small rewards for reaching a daily or early target include a massage, a nice dinner out, or a weekend getaway. Larger rewards for bigger monthly targets include a home upgrade, a car purchase, or a month working from another part of the world. [L1039.026]
- Reward small wins promptly, since appreciating progress keeps effort going and stops you from taking results for granted. Rewards do not need to be expensive; their purpose is to show that your work is leading somewhere, and you should not spend all your earnings on them. [L1039.027]
- Reward yourself for every measurable parameter of success, such as completing a task or reaching a milestone. Rewards trick the mind into taking action and keep the business moving forward. [L1043.011]
- Gamify hard or boring work by timing how quickly it can be finished or setting a challenge, which makes progress feel like leveling up. [L1181.011]

### Measuring progress and celebrating wins

- Downplaying your wins makes you stop seeking what works. Measure progress, celebrate measurable gains, and then keep improving. [L0378.110]

## Learn efficiently and turn learning into action

### Start with execution, not study

- Consistent execution matters more than talent or tools, so a beginner who follows a clear action plan tends to outpace people who only consume content [L0491.001].
- Knowing the right product, marketing and customer service methods is necessary but not sufficient. The deciding factor is whether you apply that knowledge step by step [L0419.002].
- Reading or watching material has no value on its own. Results come only from applying each method yourself with full attention. Beginners who consume lessons passively without implementing them usually see no progress [L1492.004].
- Learning by absorbing information alone is not enough. A student without questions should still take action on what they have learned [L0577.073].
- Learn by doing: run ads, analyze the data with mentors, watch recorded sessions and test multiple products, instead of only studying theory [L0440.062].
- Roughly 90% of the time you already know what needs to be done. The real bottleneck is execution, not knowledge, so more learning is rarely the answer [L1042.013].
- Marketing knowledge produces little result if the owner applies it with the wrong focus or uses time inefficiently, so execution is often the real bottleneck rather than knowledge [L0802.002].
- Tools, mentors and communities can guide a business, but the operator must actively understand what is happening in the store. Deliberate, intentional work on the business matters more than passively consuming material [L1487.010].
- Own the research for your niche and products. Doing the work yourself builds the confidence needed to act on the store and carry it through the difficult early months [L1281.123].
- Experienced operators can get traffic to live products within two weeks of choosing a niche, if they start supplier calls right away and build a demo store while waiting. This is an experienced-operator benchmark, not a beginner target [L0804.105] (2023).

### Study a course in the right order

- Follow the course sequence in order. Each step builds on the one before it, so skipping ahead causes problems later. Mark each step as complete so you always know where you stand [L0468.181] (2020).
- Do not skip modules in the order they come. Work through the training step by step and ask questions when stuck [L1112.074] (2021).
- Follow the course steps in order, and do not stop after rejections from suppliers, because rejections are normal when contacting many suppliers [L1088.089] (2020).
- Follow the build order: choose the niche, build the store, get supplier approvals, set up ads, then track analytics and respond to customers quickly. Starting ads before the products are live wastes money [L1111.173] (2020).
- The checklist is a fixed order of phases: community and planning, legal setup, niche and research, store build, suppliers, conversion, traffic, then automation. Each phase depends on the one before it, so tasks should be completed in sequence [L0393.026].
- Do not start building the store before the product has been chosen. Work in a fixed order (product research first, then store build, then ads) and move to the next step only after the homework for the current step is finished [L1017.004].
- Store readiness and approvals: the store must be ready before the launch, but suppliers approve on a step-by-step basis. Getting approvals, uploading products and then adding traffic should happen in that order [L1094.116] (2023).
- Each module ends with action tasks that must be finished before the next module starts, and they should be done in sequence. The processes behind them are the ones used for every new store, so skipping a task makes the later steps harder to follow [L1056.024].
- If the earlier brainstorming steps were skipped, go back and complete them before adding ideas to the niche list. The list is meant to build on that earlier work [L1056.025].
- Follow the course program fully instead of freestyling. Watching only part of the material and building your own ideas (such as nine different stores) before reaching mass success is likely to fail [L1394.015].
- Discipline is essential in e-commerce. Adhere to the prescribed process precisely and avoid deviating from it before you have achieved significant results [L1394.017].
- Three requirements for success: follow the video library, take part in the community, and take action on what you learn. Combine these with the right mindset [L1394.016].
- Choosing only the parts of a training program that feel appealing and skipping the rest, or watching only part of each lesson, usually leads to no results. Work through every step in order and do not shortcut the material [L1492.002].
- Watch every video in the program, not just the ones that seem known. The only exception for beginners is to skip videos tagged as advanced. Skipping material means acting on incomplete information, which wastes time and advertising money [L0444.014].
- Do not skip any module, even if the content looks familiar, because skipped material can lead to costly mistakes later. Treat the action tasks as assignments, since each one builds on the previous ones [L0491.004].
- Learning the technical side of building a store is useful even when someone else builds the store for you, because the training shows how each part is set up [L0468.183] (2020).
- Learning the basics of Shopify yourself is necessary even if you hire people, because you need to run and understand your own store. Building a first site yourself can bring early sales, and paying a professional later once money is available is a valid path [L0921.013].
- Building a Shopify store is not technically complex, although it can be tedious. A beginner can complete it by following the instructions in order [L1060.007].
- Do not spend time learning highly technical details of e-commerce before the store is live. Focus on proven steps and learn the rest as the store runs [L0806.098] (2024).
- Learn the foundations of paid traffic before collecting tips, because specific tips expire as ad types and interfaces change. Without a strong foundation, more tips do not improve results [L0510.059].
- Study the theory and the psychology behind paid search campaigns before building campaigns. Rewatch learning material as often as needed until the concepts are clear [L0713.013].
- Pay-per-click advertising is new to most beginners, so it is normal for the concept to take time and practice to understand. Confusion at the start is common and does not indicate a personal shortcoming [L0713.012].
- Watch the full training from start to finish before setting up or configuring anything, and do not pause to build things during the first pass. Then go back through it step by step, because seeing the whole system first makes later decisions more intentional [L0491.002].

### Choose your starting track

- Choose your starting track by stage: a brand new business starts with the build track, while an owner with a launched business starts with the grow track. Do not work through the grow track before your first business is launched, unless you are opening a second or third business [L0545.001] (2021-22).
- Build track, in this order [L0545.002] (2021-22):
  1. Welcome
  2. Find Your Market
  3. Build Your Website
  4. Secure Your Supplies
  5. Launch Your Business
  6. Running Your Business Day to Day

  Watch each video in sequence without skipping or jumping around, and complete the action item each video asks for. Ignore the grow track and the smaller courses until the business is launched, because the order is designed to avoid unnecessary difficulty [L0545.002].
- The build track has 6 modules, and the grow track had 12 modules at the time of recording. Expect to spend at least about one month in the build track [L0545.003] (2021-22).
- Conflicting timing for the build phase: the source above says at least about one month [L0545.003], while another source says a high-ticket beginner should expect the build phase to take a few weeks of steady work rather than a few days. Fast results come from consistent daily effort, not from compressing many hours into one session. Pressure to finish quickly tends to cause burnout and dropout [L1496.001] (2021-22). Use the one-month figure as a planning minimum and the few-weeks figure as the expected range for steady work.
- When building a first or second high-ticket dropshipping business, stay focused on the build phase and ignore other content until the store is ready to launch. Work through the instructions in order, and only later move on to growing the business [L1490.001] (2021-22).
- A high-ticket dropshipping store is ready to sell products once every action step has been completed in sequence. Follow the step-by-step instructions exactly as given, and complete the action step that ends most lessons before moving on [L1490.002] (2021-22).
- Grow track for a business launched within the last 12 months [L0545.004] (2021-22):
  1. Start with module 1.
  2. Work through the modules in chronological order (2, 3, 4, and so on).
  3. Take the action item from each video before moving on.

  There is no time limit or deadline, so move forward in small, regular steps [L0545.004].
- An owner with several years of business experience should start with the module covering their biggest known weakness, because that is where the largest knowledge gaps usually are. Afterwards, the remaining modules can be worked through, since even stronger areas can be improved [L0545.005] (2021-22).
- Beginners new to online business should first learn the basics and choose a niche, and they do not need to finish all training before launching. Work through the action tasks at the end of each training section in order to make progress [L0356.063] (2019).
- The 21-day launch plan and the 7-week quick start guide do not replace the course content. They only guide you through the same course lessons at different paces, so choose one or go self-paced [L0804.104] (2023).

### Watch-to-action ratio

- Watching videos should account for less than 50% of the total time spent on the program. Each module needs at least twice the length of its video in additional time for action and hands-on implementation [L0444.004].
- Budget at least one hour of learning time for a 45-minute video, because pausing, rewinding and note-taking take time [L0444.012].
- Students who make progress are the ones who put full effort into the product pitch and keep testing, not those who finish tasks in an hour and move on. Many quit before the first or second task [L0691.029].
- Action items that run over a set period, such as 30 days, should be started immediately after the related lesson and carried out over that whole period before expecting results [L0802.005].

| Element | Guidance | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Video share of total time | Under 50% | L0444.004 |
| Action time per module | At least 2 × video length | L0444.004 |
| Learning time per 45-min video | At least 1 hour | L0444.012 |

### Process each video with notes

- Take handwritten notes on every video, because the material is too dense to retain without them. Use a physical notebook as the reference sheet for the action phase [L0444.010].
- Have a physical notebook ready before starting any e-commerce training, because handwritten notes are needed from the first video onward. Do not begin the material without one [L0444.021].
- Process each video with notes [L0444.011]:
  1. Watch a section and pause when needed.
  2. Rewind and re-watch any part that is unclear.
  3. Write notes on what was taught.
  4. Pause again, go back and verify the notes against the video.
  5. Re-read the notes and check that everything makes sense; if something does not click, go back and repeat.
  6. Allocate time for this whole process rather than watching everything in one go.
- Notes help the action phase because they let you apply information you have already processed and translated into your own words, which is faster than re-watching videos to find a detail [L0444.013].
- Learning sticks better when you write your own key points and summaries of what you read or practice than when you rely on ready-made summaries. Doing the analysis yourself is part of the learning. Review the key points again after a few days [L1206.050].
- Rewatching the course a second or third time usually reveals details missed earlier, because your practical experience changes what you take from each lesson [L0804.106] (2023).
- Learning a complex platform such as Google Ads takes repeated study, with pauses and notes over several days. Taking the time to set it up correctly is better than running a weak setup while you learn [L0961.085] (2020).
- Learning Google Ads and Google Shopping concepts can take a beginner considerable time to understand, so repeatedly re-watching lessons and performing the setup steps alongside the videos is a sensible way to learn them [L1065.006] (2021-22).
- Learning Google Ads takes time, and people grasp the platform at different speeds. Persistence and regular practice lead to better understanding, even when the interface feels confusing at the start [L1228.009] (2021-22).
- Reaching real proficiency in Google Ads takes months to years of continuous work. Each round of optimization makes the next one faster and more effective [L1078.001].
- Rewatch and revisit traffic knowledge repeatedly, especially once traffic and sales start, to find improvements in each channel [L0597.014].

### Study and action in alternating blocks

- Do not mix watching and acting in the same block by pausing a video to carry out tasks, and do not watch the course like entertainment. Watch with full focus, because the material is dense and passive viewing wastes money [L0444.009].
- Pace the study so that you learn new material and revisit older material at regular intervals. Going too fast (binge watching) or too slow both reduce retention and make earlier content hard to recall [L0444.007].
- Plan study and action in alternating blocks [L0444.008]:
  1. Study a video block on one day, focused and taking notes, without binge watching multiple modules in a row.
  2. Extract as much information as possible into your notes during the study block.
  3. On the next day, take action on what you learned the previous day.
  4. If you have a long session, such as four hours, split it into two hours of study, a short break, and two hours of action on the material from the first block.
- Work through the course one lesson at a time and complete the action item at the end of each lesson before moving on. Do not binge-watch all videos first and only then start building the business [L1496.002].
- Learning method for a sequenced course with action items in each lesson [L0802.004]:
  1. Watch the first video.
  2. Complete the action item from that video right away, and start it immediately if it is a multi-day task.
  3. Only after the action is set up, move on to the next video.
  4. Complete that video's action item, then continue to the next video.
  5. Repeat until all videos and action items are done, working slower than a normal course pace.

### Learn in short units

- Long learning material is easier to act on when split into short, actionable units of about 5 to 10 minutes each, where each unit ends with a concrete task that can be checked off. Survey feedback from a community of about 72 members favored this format [L0555.006].
- Schedule focused blocks for the first viewing with no distractions and no multitasking. Take notes as you go, especially on product research and ads, and aim to finish the first full viewing on days 1 and 2 of the plan [L0491.003].

### Daily small targets

- Schedule daily study time, even 30 minutes, and move through the course modules in order, completing each module's action tasks before starting the next [L0665.117] (2022).
- Make a little progress each day by following the steps in order, pausing for each action, asking questions when stuck, and submitting the niche for verification [L0963.149] (2022).
- Progress that happens every single day adds up to the goal, so small, completable daily tasks are more useful than large, vague plans [L0466.007] (2018).
- Instead of looking at a whole course module at once, plan one small daily target, such as finishing the first four lessons of module one, and ignore the rest until that target is done [L0466.006] (2018).
- Skill comes from repeated practice, and early results are always weaker than later ones. Daily focused practice on one core skill, such as hand-copying strong sales letters for an hour, compounds into a valuable ability [L0378.109].
- Daily reading of business-relevant non-fiction supports personal growth and business performance, and works like structured learning without a teacher [L0705.003].
- Watching more than one lesson a day is fine when time allows, but the goal is to finish the material, not to hit a daily count. Do not add unnecessary pressure to the schedule [L1496.012].

### 21-day launch plan

- A 21-day launch plan gives a clear daily focus, which prevents overwhelm and distraction when the full course or store build feels too large [L0466.005] (2018).
- A 21-day launch format gives a community one action task per day, posted with a daily video, with questions answered and short screen recordings shared. Covered steps included niche selection, store build, supplier approvals and starting traffic [L0373.016] (older, 2019-2022 source).
- Full-time launch of a store and its ads can be done in about 21 days working eight hours a day, following a step-by-step launch plan. Most people need 30 minutes to two hours per day after launch [L0468.179] (2020).
- The 21-day launch plan involves a daily task that takes between about 5 minutes and 90 minutes. Following it each day means the first store can be launched within 21 days [L0967.094] (2020).
