- A 21-day quick start is possible for a beginner, but it requires asking questions quickly when stuck, rather than spending days on one step [L0966.102] (2025).
- A checklist for the business is the action tasks at the end of each training module. Do not move to the next module until all action tasks are complete [L0967.093] (2020).

Timing reference, as stated by different sources:

| Plan | Time | Daily load | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Full-time launch | About 21 days, 8 hours a day | 8 hours | L0468.179 |
| Part-time launch, after launch | Ongoing | 30 min to 2 hours | L0468.179 |
| 21-day plan daily task | 21 days | 5 to 90 minutes | L0967.094 |
| Experienced operator | Traffic within 2 weeks of niche choice | Not stated | L0804.105 |

### 7-day launch checklist

- In e-commerce, a well-run first week aims to reach a store that is ready to launch within seven days [L0491.001].
- Seven-day launch checklist, in order [L0491.008]:
  1. Watch the full training from start to finish without setting anything up, aiming to finish on days 1 and 2.
  2. Go back through each module and complete its action tasks, listed in that module's linked document, before moving on to the next module.
  3. Go through the #resources and #tools Discord channels, introduce yourself, and register for the weekly live call.
  4. While still going through the training, research at least 15 product ideas with Winning Hunter, looking for high emotional demand, strong margins and a wide audience.
  5. Fill in the product research template with pricing, estimated margins, competitor links and audience notes, and submit it through the Discord ticket channel.
  6. Wait for the product to be approved before building anything.
  7. Build the store with Elixir by following the build guide, and optimize images, copy, urgency elements and trust badges on the product page.
  8. Submit the finished store for review via ticket before it goes live.
  9. After store approval, launch Meta ads on Facebook and Instagram using the module's campaign structure, creatives and recommended testing budget, and track the numbers from day one.
- Complete the action tasks listed in each module's linked document before moving on to the next module. Do not advance until the current module's tasks are finished [L0491.005].
- Research for a new product: invest about one week of research, roughly 8 hours per day (minimum 1 to 2 days), into understanding the customer avatar before building the store and launching ads [L1158.021].

### 7-week and multi-month pace

- Plan to finish the full program over several months, not one month. A deep program with many hours of content and action cannot be completed quickly, and expecting that leads to demotivation [L0444.006].
- A structured plan with one lesson per day over six to ten weeks, followed by action on each lesson, keeps progress steady. While a student waits for the next call, give them a set list of tasks or videos to work through so they do not stall [L1017.007].
- The 7-week quick start guide is the same course at a slower pace than the 21-day plan, and it does not replace the lessons [L0804.104] (2023).
- Progress timelines differ by source: the 7-day checklist targets a store ready to launch in a week [L0491.001], the 21-day plan targets a first store launched in three weeks [L0967.094], and the full program takes several months [L0444.006]. Choose the pace that matches your available hours and the depth of the material.

### Avoid over-consumption

- Do not consume training content like YouTube videos without taking action. Excessive consumption of educational material without implementation wastes time better spent on execution [L0037.008].
- Consuming large amounts of marketing training without implementing it in the business wastes the owner's time and delivers no growth [L0802.003] (2021-22).
- Endless learning, such as scrolling social feeds, bookmarking posts, and watching videos, feels productive but is a distraction from the risky execution work that actually moves the business forward. It is the biggest trap for people who avoid risk [L1042.012].
- Skipping action items because they seem unimportant or because the owner wants to get to tactics faster is a serious error, since the action items build on each other and are designed to set up the owner's work [L0802.006] (2021-22).
- Learning by absorbing information alone is not enough. Turn learning into action at each step (see the action rules above) [L0577.073].
- Do not learn the advanced details of e-commerce before the store is live. Most lessons cover the consistent core steps, so the owner learns the rest through real questions and experience rather than needing outside marketing programs first [L1094.114] (2023).
- Running a store as a side business can come with a fear of not knowing enough about Google Ads or optimization. The owner learns the rest through real questions and experience [L1094.114] (2023).
- Most people who buy online business training do not finish it, so the success rate is not measured. Consistent work and asking questions are the strongest predictors of results [L0806.099] (2024).

### Avoid shiny objects and extra courses

- While you are implementing a business plan, remove external distractions: do not buy additional courses, educational products or shiny objects that pull attention away from execution [L0419.006] (2021-22).
- Hopping from one business idea to the next (shiny object syndrome) stops progress. A beginner should commit to one business model and one strategy and run it through before judging it [L0631.001].
- Extra paid programs and upgrades are optional growth tools for a store that is already profitable. They are not required to build a successful dropshipping store [L0359.109].
- Trick-based methods can work for a time, while fundamentals hold up over the long term. Focus on fundamentals such as branding, creatives and ad basics rather than on individual tricks that may stop working [L0363.077].
- Ad platforms change their features and rules almost every week, so no one can claim to have fully mastered them. Keep up with changes, but base decisions on the fundamentals of marketing and sales, which stay the same [L1213.016].
- Use a proven set of techniques instead of trying every technique in the books. Experienced writers keep a handful of techniques that work consistently, so start with those few and test others only when you have time [L1370.111].

### Test products before judging

- Test multiple products and analyze the results with mentors instead of judging the business after a single attempt [L0440.062].
- Students who make progress are the ones who put full effort into the product pitch and keep testing [L0691.029].
- Research at least 15 product ideas with Winning Hunter, looking for high emotional demand, strong margins and a wide audience [L0491.008].
- Learn a new product category with a few hours of basic overview videos, then learn most of it by running ads and answering real customers. Each unanswered question should be passed to the supplier and then added to your site [L1277.117] (2020).
- Learn from competitors and other sellers' errors through their reviews and experiences. This is safer than learning only through your own mistakes [L0962.143] (2021).
- Study documented results from people who already succeeded at the task instead of repeating their costly trial and error. Reusing proven methods saves years of testing and money [L0340.074].
- Writing a strong sales letter is a learnable skill, not a talent: the mechanics are repeatable, and the business owner who knows the customer has a major advantage over a freelancer who must research them from scratch. Begin by writing blocks of copy and stacking them, rather than aiming for a finished letter at once [L1370.109].
- Read the book 100 Million Offers by Alex Hermosi to learn how to build strong offers [L1220.039].
- Ready, Fire, Aim by Michael Masterson is a book on direct response business thinking. Read it if you plan to build a private label product line [L0413.043].

### Discipline and following the process

- Discipline is essential in e-commerce. Adhere to the prescribed process precisely and avoid deviating from it before you have achieved significant results [L1394.017].
- Follow the course program fully instead of freestyling [L1394.015].
- Do not skip any module, and do not deviate from the sequence until the current module's action tasks are done [L0491.004], [L0491.005].
- Choose the same discipline for every module: start with the first module that applies to your stage, and finish its action tasks before the next [L0545.001], [L0545.002].
- Do not stop after rejections. Keep following the steps and contacting suppliers [L1088.089] (2020).

### Continuous learning

- Treat constant learning as part of the job: follow digital marketing groups, ask questions and answer questions in forums. Marketers who keep their skills current stay ahead of those who stop learning [L0489.008].
- The willingness to keep learning is the most valuable asset an entrepreneur can have, because nobody can take it away and it can carry you a long way. Success in this business requires constantly adapting and picking up new skills [L0926.002].
- The most valuable skill in dropshipping is a willingness to learn, because it cannot be taken away and it carries you through changing conditions. Expect platforms and rules to change and keep updating your knowledge [L1441.004].
- Investing in ongoing education, such as podcasts, reading, a mastermind group, a monthly local business breakfast and yearly conferences, is recommended. Even one useful idea per session can make a large difference to the business [L1437.035].
- When learning a platform such as Shopify from scratch, repeated basic questions in community groups and rewatching setup tutorials are a normal part of progress. Keep returning to the business goal rather than stopping when the technical steps feel overwhelming [L1437.040].
- Learning web development works best through a mix of YouTube tutorials, free online programming courses, and books, depending on whether the learner prefers video or reading. Consistent practice through trial and error builds the skill [L0709.038].
- Learn people deeply. Study human psychology and people, because this knowledge directly improves your business decisions, marketing, and customer relationships [L0007.014].
- Copywriting is closely tied to general marketing. Reading classic copywriting and marketing books improves a marketer's overall skill, not only their writing, when the ideas are applied in practice [L1523.006].

### Reading routine

- Turn daily reading into lasting knowledge with this order [L0705.002]:
  1. Read non-fiction for at least 30 minutes.
  2. Highlight the key passages.
  3. Take written notes on the main ideas.
  4. Discuss what you read with other people so the concepts stay in memory.
- Daily reading of business-relevant non-fiction supports personal growth and business performance, and works like structured learning without a teacher [L0705.003].

## Decide fast and launch imperfectly

### Overthinking and analysis paralysis

- Overthinking paralyses execution. Think less and do more, rely on coaches, and solve problems as they arise during the work instead of planning everything perfectly upfront. [L0037.001]
- Analysis paralysis is best broken by taking action, even with an imperfect first version, because things can be corrected once they are live. *(Older, 2022.)* [L1092.106]
- Progress comes from moving forward and correcting mistakes along the way, which works better than waiting for a perfect first attempt. Making mistakes is normal in business. *(Older, 2022.)* [L1092.105]
- Business decisions are made from current knowledge plus instinct. Waiting for 100% confidence before acting is a common trap because that confidence never arrives. Move forward with what you know and be ready to pivot when results show a different path. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0358.013]
- Overthinking the niche choice holds many beginners back. Pick a niche that passes the criteria and start the work rather than waiting for certainty. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0962.138]
- Overthinking a product costs money and loses opportunities. Choose products with proof of sales, run ads, and let the Facebook data decide instead of your own assumptions. [L1161.045]
- Overanalysing the niche decision makes people go in circles and doubt their own reasoning. For a first business, this lack of progress can keep people stuck for months. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1059.005]
- Searching for the perfect niche, product or customer leads to endless second-guessing, sometimes lasting weeks or months. This delay causes frustration and often ends with the person giving up. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1040.003]
- Do not keep cycling between unsure ideas without deciding. Endless re-evaluation delays building the business. Once research is complete, either confirm a market or get an outside check. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1058.007]
- Use a niche verification tool for external validation, get the green light, then move forward. Overthinking prevents progress. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0268.060]
- Research-heavy work takes time, and taking longer to choose a niche is acceptable if it avoids a blind launch. Do not let indecision stop you indefinitely. Once an idea is validated, act on it. *(Older, 2019.)* [L1002.072]
- The longer you delay a decision, the less likely you are to act on it at all. Choose a viable niche, start the store and learn from real results. *(Older, 2018.)* [L1274.014]
- Founders often keep many ideas open out of fear of missing other opportunities. Committing to one anchor category and a few concrete steps is usually what makes progress start. [L1016.004]

### Perfectionism and waiting for the perfect launch

- Perfectionism kills progress in business. [L1040.004]
- Waiting for a perfect store, perfect creatives or perfect timing before launching is usually resistance disguised as perfectionism. A launch produces real market data that no preparation can replace, so the sooner a basic version is live and measurable, the sooner the decisions that matter can be made. [L0625.001]
- Delaying a launch indefinitely while polishing details keeps the business at zero results and hides the real bottleneck, which is usually the lack of live feedback. Treat the feeling of not being ready as a signal to define a minimum launch version and set a fixed go-live date. [L0625.002]
- Perfectionism delays the collection of real customer data. A store with its core elements working should go live. Launch day is only the starting point, and the store is then improved continuously from its own data. [L1446.001]
- Waiting for every supplier approval and a perfect performance score before going live postpones the only thing a launch is meant to deliver: data from real visitors. The goal at launch is learning, not a large first-day sales spike. [L1446.002]
- The main reason to launch early is to learn what customers search for, how they compare the store with competitors and what they think of it. These insights come only from real visits and cannot be fully replaced by theoretical optimisation. [L1446.003]
- Time cannot be recovered. Waiting months to pick the perfect niche, start ads or call a supplier costs more than launching and refining. Launch the store early instead of over-planning. [L1515.003]
- Overplanning can stop action. A minimum viable product that goes live and is refined from real customer feedback is often better than waiting for perfection. Balance speed with quality by launching early and improving over time. [L0552.046]
- Over-complicating the brand document delays the launch. Write down as much information as possible quickly, using what you already know about the product and customer. [L1044.033]
- Testing in dropshipping means the brand is launched early and refined later. Waiting for a complete brand before going live slows learning. [L1044.034]
- Test every element, and do not wait for a perfect product or relationship before launching. If a test fails, learn from it, apologise where needed and move on. *(Older, 2020s; undated in source.)* [L0790.086]
- Templates let beginners reach the market faster. Start with ready-made templates, such as a landing page or ad template, and learn design and psychology while ads run. Analysing why a live ad works builds skills in parallel. Waiting until all learning is finished before launching is not sustainable. [L1348.028]
- Pursuing a perfect niche is a common trap. Choose a niche that passes all tests, start building and improve through real traffic and sales. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0967.095]
- Do not chase the perfect niche before building. Even imperfect stores often beat expectations, and each store builds experience for the next one. *(Older, 2021.)* [L1112.075]
- A perfect market does not exist. Every market idea has downsides as well as a reasonable number of upsides. The goal of niche selection is to find a good market, not an ideal one. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1040.002]
- When selecting a market, the aim is to identify good ideas and avoid bad ones, not to find perfect ideas. Keep this standard in mind throughout the selection process. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1040.005]
- It is hard to predict whether a niche will become a very large store or a modest one. Screen niches efficiently and start instead of researching endlessly. *(Older, 2025.)* [L1116.046]

### Time limits on niche choice

- Niche selection must stay within a strict time limit because perfectionism kills progress. It should normally take no more than one week, and often only one to two days. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1040.004]
- Market and product selection should be completed within a matter of days, and some people finish it the same day. Do not allow it to stretch into months. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1059.006]
- Set a fixed deadline for niche research, such as three days or one week, and choose a niche on that date no matter what. This prevents endless research that never leads to a launch. *(Older, 2018.)* [L1085.096]
- Use the forum to explore niche selection before choosing, but do not stay in research mode for long. Balance learning with a firm decision date. *(Older, 2018.)* [L1085.099]
- Moving through market selection quickly matters because the goal is to launch and start earning as early as possible. Getting stuck at this stage is the main risk to avoid. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1040.008]
- Market selection is about choosing a good-enough niche quickly so you can move on to building the business. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1040.009]
- Many niches and products can work, so a choice that meets the criteria is better than waiting for a better option. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1059.007]
- If niche selection stalls for a long time, narrow the options to about five serious candidates and commit to one. Continued research without a decision delays the store more than a reasonable choice would. *(Older, 2018.)* [L1109.068]
- Do not spend time on brainstorming to the point of stalling, but also do not stop at a few ideas. See the research depth section below for list sizes. [L1056.022]
- Ad launch on a single product: taking two weeks or more is too slow. Research properly and launch within about two days, then make decisions from data. *(Source D 2026.)* [L1038.003]

**Break a niche decision block (procedure)**

1. Take your top two niches from the research list.
2. Set a specific deadline for choosing, such as a date only a few days away.
3. Submit the top two for niche verification if you need outside input.
4. Choose one and start building the store.

[L0941.098]

**Time budgets by stage**

| Stage | Budget in source | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Niche selection | Normally no more than one week; often one to two days; some finish the same day | [L1040.004, L1059.006] |
| Niche selection (fixed deadline) | Three days or one week, then choose regardless | [L1085.096] |
| Single-product ads | Launch within about two days; two weeks or more is too slow | [L1038.003] |
| Full product chain (idea to test-ready product, including research, recipes, covers, images and PDF) | Three to four days | [L1131.056] |
| First high-ticket store, setup to launch | About 30 calendar days; some need longer, which does not predict failure *(older, 2021-22)* | [L0438.005] |
| First store launch deadline | 30 to 60 days; the 21-day timeline is a fast option for those who want to move quickly *(older, 2023)* | [L0964.144] |
| Launch timeline by experience | A 21-day launch is far more intense than a seven-week one; both reach the same result; 21 days suits people who already know some technology *(older, 2021)* | [L1279.088] |

### Niche research: depth and sources

- Study how to identify profitable niches before filling in the niche list, so the list starts from a viable position rather than from unchecked ideas. [L0591.008]
- Complete the niche research steps yourself before submitting ideas for verification, so the verification has good ideas to work with. The team can then help you proceed or pivot. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0551.062]
- Do not rush niche research. Thorough research gives the confidence to push through the early work and spend on ads. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0803.090]
- Research suppliers and niches thoroughly before launching; extra upfront research is good practice. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0834.109]
- Build enough confidence in a niche before launching, by checking competition and brand data first. Going in with a try-it-and-see mindset makes every bump look like proof that the niche does not work. *(Older, 2019.)* [L0467.026]
- Market research can take several months. If a niche has many failures, keep pivoting from the closest candidate rather than starting from zero. *(Older, 2023.)* [L0855.173]
- Niche selection is a repeated process of brainstorming ideas and then testing each against the criteria. Retest ideas before committing, since the logic often takes several passes to click. *(Older, 2023.)* [L0970.159]
- Do not rely on a single niche idea when competition is high. Be sure of the niche before committing by using verification and competitor checks. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0969.107]
- Market finding is an open, exploratory phase. Generate many ideas without strict limits; evaluate them against criteria afterwards. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1058.005]
- Do not stop the brainstorm at a few ideas, such as 10. The choice of niche can decide whether the business succeeds, so a larger list gives more options. *(Older, 2020s; undated in source.)* [L1056.022]
- Build a list of 50 or more researched niches. It supports future stores, because a second or third store can be chosen from the list without starting the search from zero. [L1056.023]
- Some ideas are better considered for later stores. A first store should be simple and use the proven process, while more complex brand or seasonal ideas can wait for stores two to ten. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0967.096]
- An outside review of a niche is optional. A niche that clearly passes all four criteria can go ahead without it, while a niche with uncertain data benefits from a second set of eyes before you invest in building a store. [L1080.009]
- If you are unsure whether a niche fits, get an outside review of your criteria and reasoning instead of looping on it alone. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1059.007]
- Get an outside review of the shortlist before setup begins, since this can reduce uncertainty. *(Older, 2024.)* [L0360.003]
- Finding the market and the right products is usually the hardest early step in starting a high-ticket dropshipping business. Once that is settled, most remaining work is setup and execution. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0912.007]
- The market selection process must be done personally, because the ability to find and evaluate a market alone is needed later when growing a business without guidance. Someone who needs step-by-step instructions for this first stage will likely struggle with growth. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0996.014]
- Before choosing, study the criteria and identify the largest possible set of candidates. Because the top criteria are discussed in the next section, use them to filter the list. [L0591.008]
- Product research in dropshipping is no longer reliable when based on random trending products and luck. Success depends on a deliberate process: good selection criteria, differentiation from existing sellers and a deep understanding of the target audience. [L1158.001]
- Testing a wide range of interests is useful, since the right interest cannot be known in advance. Choose interests from what buyers do in their lives, not just from the product itself. [L1439.048]

### Niche criteria and tiebreakers

- Competition occurs at the product level within a niche, not the niche level. Multiple stores can succeed in the same niche if they differ in product selection and pricing. [L0255.068]
- To judge whether a niche is too competitive:
  1. Go to lesson 2.9, "How much competition is too much competition".
  2. Apply the framework to the top-selling products in that niche.
  3. Check whether the top competitors have healthy profit margins.
  4. Decide based on product-level competition, not overall niche saturation.

  [L0255.069]
- At the competition research stage, the only question is whether a niche is too crowded or too empty. Working out how to stand out comes later, once the niche has passed the research criteria. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0896.027]
- Do not choose or avoid a niche purely because of size or weight. Calculate shipping costs properly using Freight Center. Large, heavy items are viable if margins support them. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0249.085]
- Judge a niche by the criteria and by whether you can see margin, not by fear of big or heavy products. A good niche with high-ticket items can be profitable even when the product is large. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0664.083]
- A basic low-margin, low-price product such as a plain t-shirt cannot support paid advertising. Higher-priced products leave room for ads and profit. Replace low-priced niches with higher-priced ones rather than trying to fix them. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0468.182]
- Sell only what you can handle. If you cannot take on the customer service load of expensive items, start in a range you can manage and move up later. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0468.184]
- If a niche has few products but meets the other criteria, it is not an automatic no-go. Start it only if you are confident you can get enough supplier approvals to make money. *(Older, 2018.)* [L1085.098]
- Choose a niche that passes all criteria and whose products keep selling for years, so the store has no natural end date. You can sell the store later if you stop running it. *(Older, 2023.)* [L0934.189]
- Choose a recent product with broad market appeal rather than a heavily saturated one, because saturated products need a strong marketing angle to stand out. Start with low investment and optimise the funnel before spending on branding. [L1377.048]
- Pick products based on market data and proof, not personal taste, intuition or a product that merely looks appealing. Beginners who choose by feeling take on the full risk of an unproven item. [L1174.001]
- Personal passion for a product category is one of the weakest criteria for choosing what to sell. It should rank far below market opportunity and be treated as a minor factor, not a deciding one. [L0924.001]
- Choosing a product only because it matches a hobby or long-standing interest leads to pursuing low-potential opportunities. Effort, time and capital then go into a niche that will not grow. [L0924.006]
- You do not need to be an expert in a niche to start selling it. You learn the product details by answering real customer questions and checking the answers with the supplier. *(Older, 2025.)* [L0972.001]
- Niche expertise is not required. Many successful stores were started without experience in the product area, and learning happens while building. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0665.118]
- You do not need to be an expert in the niche you eventually choose, and you do not need to be passionate about the product. The goal is to become interested in the business, and knowledge of the niche grows over time. [L1056.021]
- A store can be launched while the owner is still learning the product, because customer questions teach what they need to know. Being willing to learn as you go is what matters. *(Older, 2019.)* [L0356.056]
- Once the data checks are done, personal excitement about selling the products day to day through the website is the guide for choosing between approved niches. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0490.028]

**Conflict on passion:** Passion is a minor factor overall [L0924.001] and a hobby is a mistake to choose on [L0924.006]. Excitement breaks ties only between niches that have already passed the data checks [L0490.028].

### Committing to one niche

- When several niches are approved, the expected chance of success is roughly equal. Committing to one approved niche matters more than finding a slightly better one. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0490.027]
- Make the niche decision in one sitting if possible, or sleep on it for one night at most. Then move on without spending more time on the choice. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0490.029]
- Second-guessing the chosen niche after committing wastes time. Once the decision is made, lock it in and move to the next phase. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L0490.030]
- A niche can be changed later. It is reasonable to narrow the choice to the top two or three candidates and then commit to the strongest one. *(Older, 2024.)* [L0360.003]
- Be sure of the niche before committing. Use verification and competitor checks, and do not keep relying on a single niche idea when competition is high. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0969.107]
- Avoid shiny-object syndrome. Focus on one niche where you can build a reputation, rather than chasing many unrelated niches. [L0789.051]
- Founders who keep many ideas open should commit to one anchor category and a few concrete steps. [L1016.004]
- Build one store and make it profitable before starting another. Fixing one mistake once is far cheaper than repeating it across several stores. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0852.011]
- Once the first niche is chosen, build its store before starting another. Later stores in other proven-winner niches can be added to create several income streams without shutting the first store down. *(Older, 2020s; undated in source.)* [L1079.006]
- Do not abandon a half-built store to chase a new business model that sounds appealing. Reach the current goal first, then consider the next model. [L0826.015]
- A first store should be simple and use the proven process. More complex brand or seasonal ideas can wait for stores two to ten. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0967.096]
- Continuous improvement is better than endless research. If a first store's niche is not perfect, it can still be profitable and provide a learning experience. While it generates profit, build a second store with better niche research. *(Older, 2020.)* [L0268.059]

### When to persist and when to pivot

- A niche that passes all criteria deserves persistence. Improve the store before abandoning it, and only sell or rebuild once it has had a fair chance. *(Older, 2021.)* [L0247.017]
- When many failures appear in one niche, keep pivoting from the closest candidate rather than starting from zero. *(Older, 2023.)* [L0855.173]
- Use both rules together: persist with a niche that passes the criteria and improve it; pivot to the next candidate when the niche shows repeated failures. [L0247.017, L0855.173]
- Working on the store through improvements before abandoning, and pivoting only when the closest candidate keeps failing, are both needed to avoid two traps: quitting too early and researching forever. [L0247.017, L0855.173, L0268.059]
- Niche research can be repeated, and a candidate that fails should be retested before it is dropped. *(Older, 2023.)* [L0970.159]

### Launch before you know everything

- Build and validate the store with at least one paying customer before investing time in advanced scaling strategies and optimisation content. Foundational setup, launch and first sale take priority over growth tactics. Return to advanced material once core operations work. *(Older, 2015.)* [L0065.001]
- Use the first sale as the checkpoint: once the core operations are functional, growth tactics are the next priority. [L0065.001]
- Start with the basic store and proven niche approach rather than many products at once. A structured sequence of setup, supplier approval and then traffic is the reliable path. *(Older, 2023.)* [L1283.103]
- Do not spend time on paperwork or heavy setup before the store has anything to show. Start with what is needed to sell and add structures later. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0963.150]
- Build a store before wholesale prices are known, since calculations for each supplier can be done once the costs are confirmed. Starting without the calculation gives you no data to decide what to sell. *(Older, 2022.)* [L0854.110]
- Do not rush the store to completion either. Complete niche selection, supplier approvals and the store build before the launch month, so traffic can start as soon as the store opens. Work on the store in small daily steps rather than leaving it to the final weeks. *(Older, 2025.)* [L0667.088]
- Test and iterate: the brand is refined after launch, not before. [L1044.034]

### Cash flow and lean launch

- Cash flow matters more than perfection. Focus on finding small profit margins quickly rather than building the perfect brand before launching. [L0307.027]
- Product research follows the same rule: do not wait for a winning product. Launch fast, test offers on real audiences and iterate based on data. For example, test three offer variations, keep the one with the highest CTR and conversion rate, and scale it. Speed beats perfection. [L0307.027]
- Launch the store as cheaply as possible and reinvest as it earns. Stores started with too much money behind them tended to perform worse than bootstrapped ones. *(Older, 2021.)* [L1279.086]
- Build the business in stages, with each stage tied to profitability. Even a store with modest results can already generate profit that supports the next stage. *(Older, 2025.)* [L1446.029]
- Finish niche research and verification before building a store, because a store in a weak niche wastes money. A few extra weeks of research are better than a live store that cannot sell. *(Older, 2021.)* [L1279.087]
- Choosing a launch timeline depends on available time and experience. See the time budget table above for the 21-day and seven-week options. [L1279.088]
- Start with low investment and optimise the funnel before spending on branding. [L1377.048]

### Store setup and launch steps

**First high-ticket store sequence (procedure, older, 2021-22)**

1. Set up the business (store and supplier arrangements).
2. Launch the store.
3. Get the first sales.
4. Then shift focus to growth: marketing systems and scaling the business.

[L0438.002]

**Store launch checklist (procedure, older, 2022)**

1. Secure approval from at least two suppliers.
2. Upload their products to your Shopify store.
3. Remove all demo products.
4. Test the checkout process.
5. Turn on Google Shopping ads.

Open for business and let customers find you. Continue adding suppliers and optimising in the background. [L0272.050]

**Supplier approval and launch readiness**

- The checklist requires at least two supplier approvals before launch. A launch that waits for every supplier approval is delayed, so the minimum is two approvals and the rest can follow. [L0272.050, L1446.002]
- Get enough supplier approvals to make money before starting a niche with few products. [L1085.098]
- When supplier outreach gets no response, change the call timing or approach first. Most brands can still be reached this way, so widen the niche only as a last resort. *(Older, 2023.)* [L0970.158]
- A new seller should not delay contacting suppliers out of fear of rejection. Waiting too long often leads to abandoning the niche before any outreach. *(Older, 2023.)* [L1094.115]
- Overthinking the call or feeling awkward about calling customers stops the programme from happening. These calls are positive, and customers who have bought generally like to hear from the business. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1011.016]

**Launch from the Source D self-onboarding form**

*Setting:* Work in your own copy of the self-onboarding Google Doc: File > Make a Copy. Fill in the copy so the original template stays clean. [L1268.002]

*Complete the form in this order:*

1. Answer what your business differentiator is and how you stand out from the rest, covering your products and mission.
2. List two to three things you wish every customer knew about your products.
3. Name your best-selling product. If you are unsure, check store sales by product in Shopify and paste that product's URL. If you have just started, skip this question.
4. Describe how you want your brand to be perceived by visitors, for example luxury, premium, wholesome or witty.
5. List what stops customers from buying: complaints, comments and feedback from people who viewed the website but did not purchase.
6. List influencers or authorities who work with or have used your products, including social media handles. Check Pinterest or Google to find them. Include social media influencers, actors, artists, celebrities and doctors.
7. Describe any seasonal buying patterns: the seasons when customers buy, or whether sales are year-round.

[L1268.003]

- Answer all seven self-assessment questions fully before moving on to scaling, ad production or any next stage. Skipping questions leaves gaps that later decisions depend on. [L1268.004]
- The self-onboarding questions clarify your niche, your goals and whether you are ready before you go deeper into a course or spend on ads. [L1268.011]

**Product chain and product choice**

- Plan the full chain from product idea to test-ready product, including research, recipes, covers, images and PDF, to take about three to four days. [L1131.056]
- Choose products with proof of sales, run ads, and let Facebook data decide. [L1161.045]

### Finishing steps and staying on one store

- Do not rush through setup just to look finished. Work through each step in order, using the checklist above. [L0272.050]
- Do not spend time polishing a step once its core output works; move to the next one. [L1446.001]
- Start with ready-made templates and move forward while learning in parallel. [L1348.028]

### Deadlines, time budgets and signs of being stuck

- Assign a realistic time budget to each launch step. If a step clearly takes longer than its budget, treat it as a sign of being stuck and ask a mentor, community or peers for help instead of continuing alone. [L0555.005]
- Set a launch deadline for the first store, for example 30 to 60 days, to avoid delaying the launch indefinitely. *(Older, 2023.)* [L0964.144]
- Set a fixed deadline for niche research, such as three days or one week, and choose on that date. *(Older, 2018.)* [L1085.096]
- Use the time budgets in the table above as the reference points for each stage. [L1040.004, L1038.003, L1131.056, L0438.005, L1279.088]

### Asking for help when stuck

- Market selection is about choosing a good-enough niche quickly, and seeking help along the way is encouraged, not a sign of weakness. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1040.009]
- Rely on coaches and solve problems as they arise instead of trying to plan everything alone. [L0037.001]
- Use the verification or outside review when you are stuck between two niches, but do not use it to delay the decision further. [L0941.098, L1080.009, L1059.007]
- Once research is complete, either confirm a market or get an outside check rather than cycling alone. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1058.007]

### Focus on high-leverage work

- Page speed work is not a high-leverage activity for a business owner. Once a site is near the 5-second mark, do not spend days or weeks tuning it. Move your time to core conversion work. *(Older, 2021-22.)* [L1522.040]
- Time spent on low-leverage work is time not spent on launching and learning from real customers. [L1515.003, L1446.003]

## Run tests with budgets, hypotheses and kill rules

### Testing culture and hypotheses

- No ad strategy is a silver bullet. Mentors give educated guesses based on experience, so every opinion must be tested. Treat each recommendation as a hypothesis, not a fact. [L0640.044]
- Testing produces proof for decisions, while advice from tools and mentors only gives guidelines. When you are unsure and the budget allows, test. When the budget is limited, put money where it already converts. [L1426.040]
- Treat every idea as a hypothesis and test it rather than debate it. Rejecting an untested idea should require a detailed written argument. The default is to run the test. [L1315.057]
- Treat every ad test as a hypothesis. Decide on the variable to test, keep the others constant, and accept that early numbers are an educated guess until more data arrives. [L1315.050]
- A hypothesis is a proposed explanation based on limited evidence that must be tested. Formulate it by reading the data, looking at the creative and theorising where the ad falls short. Then test that single hypothesis against a control. [L0640.045]
- Large companies test everything and do not reject ideas without evidence. Some results surprise you even though they look obvious afterwards, so test against your hypothesis rather than dismissing it. [L0640.046]
- Research and testing are the two pillars of finding a winning ad. Research shows who the audience is and what language they use. Testing shows what actually sells. [L1315.058]
- Research before building: look at the brand's ads, page and offers, then estimate its numbers with a simple spreadsheet and conservative inputs. This gives a clear view of the opportunity before you spend money. [L0524.062]
- No single product research method is perfect. Use the method that has worked in your own tests rather than copying someone else's routine exactly. [L1436.002]
- Testing as fundamentals, not hacks: no single method is perfect, and the goal is to increase your odds with more proof of concept. Judge each product by the evidence collected, not by intuition. [L0335.055]
- Constant testing is the approach. Test angles, videos and products repeatedly and keep what converts. Organic growth depends more on repeated testing than on one perfect video. [L0352.053]
- Find and test products systematically rather than repeating a few products. Repeated testing of a few products without a method wastes time and money. [L0361.049]
- Facebook ads are not a fixed science with guaranteed rules, so results must be verified with ongoing tests. Market conditions change, which makes continuous testing a standard part of the process. [L0692.045]
- Intuition and personal taste often misjudge what the market will buy. Check ideas by response, not by how good they seem. Expect to be wrong often and let results correct the plan. [L1261.134]
- A product can look weak and still sell. Trust the data over your own opinion, and test the product yourself with ads before rejecting it. [L1161.046]
- Use data, not emotion, for every decision in the ad account and on the store, including changes to ads, pages and budgets. Treat the numbers on the screen as the only signal of whether something works. [L1038.005]
- Base decisions on factual data rather than gut feeling, and keep learning from mistakes. The speaker presented analytical habits as a major advantage for founders starting in e-commerce. [L1508.034]
- Only trust an ad when its traced cost per customer is known. Assuming that a long-running ad is the best ad without checking its results wastes money, because some campaigns run on habit rather than data. [L1261.136]
- Test creatives, the landing page and the offer separately. Change one asset at a time based on its own metric rather than guessing. [L0871.087]
- Diagnose before changing anything, and change one variable at a time. If several things change together, it is impossible to know which change caused the result. [L1445.014]
- Before spending real money, test a new store setup or ad idea with a dummy store or dummy ad. Move to a live store with a real paid ad only after each step is understood. Record every test in a notebook or document so results can be compared later. [L1492.001]
- Compare each new product test with the data from earlier tests. Build a repeatable routine of testing, reading metrics and improving. Over time this routine builds the skills needed to recognise winners faster. [L1038.019]
- Small, well-chosen changes, such as title keywords, dynamic retargeting and opt-ins, can compound into large results. Focus on these levers before chasing new tactics. [L0510.060]
- Competitor optimisation happens gradually. Repeat the competitor review about every two weeks and make small improvements to the store over time instead of trying to fix everything at once. [L0894.003]
- Constant testing trains video skills such as hooks and angles. These skills transfer to the next product, so early tests are practice that makes it faster to scale a winning product later. [L1444.014]
- A seller should expect to test many ideas, and not every idea will work. Keep testing, measure the results and adjust quickly rather than waiting for a perfect plan. [L1153.078]
- Test one product at a time, but read the test result honestly. A failed test is feedback, not failure: test, refine and improve until the product or approach wins. [L0631.012]
- Two approaches exist. Invest deeply in one product that can become a brand, or test many products quickly to find a winner and then build a brand around it. [L1436.085]
- Winning in dropshipping comes from the volume and speed of testing, not from perfect products or perfect ads. A stalled result shows what does not work and brings you closer to what does. [L0887.016]

### Testing budget and capital

- Set a firm testing budget before you buy traffic for product tests. Avoid spending large amounts on tests that are not based on the research method. In the mentor's example, unfocused tests on other markets and platforms cost about $500 in total, while disciplined testing lost only about $100. [L0335.024]
- Testing requires spending money, because product results only show up after real spend. Set a fixed test budget in advance and judge the product on that data rather than on the first few clicks. [L0562.036]
- Only test with budget you can afford to lose, since results from small samples are noisy. The testing period also needs enough spend before the data can be trusted. [L0644.043]
- Set a fixed testing budget that you can afford to lose completely, and decide in advance that losing it will not upset you. Do not treat the budget as a limit that ends the business once it is spent. [L1394.004]
- Before seeing any profit, a beginner should expect to lose about $2,000 in testing, or whatever amount their budget is. The lower the starting budget, the smaller the expected loss. Write this expectation down before starting. [L1394.003]
- Losses scale with business size. A larger operation may lose $30,000 or $200,000 in testing. Losing money is a normal cost of doing business, not a sign to quit. [L1394.005]
- Giving yourself a budget for the business and quitting once it is spent is the wrong approach. Accept the testing budget as expendable and keep testing. [L1394.012]
- When testing a new product, spend no more than 50 percent of your available investment capital on the test. For example, with 5,000 USD in capital, keep at least half in reserve. [L1251.035]
- How long to test a product before judging it depends on your personal budget and how much risk you can take. Products can fail quickly, so keep spare capital rather than going all in. [L1251.036]
- Test a new product on a small budget first to see whether the market responds. Testing an untested product means risking the full budget on an unknown outcome. [L1177.050]
- Test one product per week with a budget of about 200 to 300 USD per product until there is enough data to either move on or keep trying it. [L1176.039]
- Ad spend on a product that makes no sales is learning money, not lost money. Move on after about 300 USD of spend with zero sales. [L1432.023]
- Spending profits as soon as they arrive, or switching between many new ventures, starves the core business. Keep the budget for the next product and for branding the current store. [L0363.076]
- Plan a fixed test budget and a schedule in advance for influencer campaigns, rather than spending freely. Influencer campaigns often fail, and some of the first creators contacted will not convert. [L1379.047]
- Keep the first sales only as proof. The core is a repeatable process of testing products, reading results and moving on. [L1440.053]
- Spending on research tools and courses is a trap. The main work is testing ads with real budgets: research can show ideas, but only ads prove them. [L1184.058]
- Early paid testing minimum: about 100 clicks per product is too small a sample for a firm conclusion. Treat the first 100 clicks as a learning and optimisation period. [L0856.112]
- Test early ads over an extended period (about 25 days at 10 dollars per day for a product) before dropping it, including optimisation time. Do not stop at a fixed dollar amount if sales are missing. [L0856.111]
- Test before scaling: start with a small test budget, and only scale an approach if it performs. Each ad account is different, so CBO may not work and ABO or other scaling methods may be needed. [L1335.031]
- Getting comfortable with large ad budgets is part of scaling a profitable store. Spend levels that seem extreme at the start can be necessary to grow tenfold later. [L0934.086]

### Kill rules and stop criteria

- Set kill rules for a product before you test it, and follow them. Keeping a product you like without results wastes time. A product with a strong visual wow factor but no clear problem it solves often gets views without sales. [L0501.039]
- Set cut rules before the test starts and follow them strictly. A failing product blocks budget that another product could use, while a strong product can make 5-10 sales on day one. [L0648.041]
- Stop a product that keeps losing money after a set test budget rather than hoping for a turnaround. Fixed stop rules keep the test budget from being spent on unproven products. [L0661.064]
- Set a clear kill rule before spending on a new product. If a product gets no sales after a fixed test spend and a few days of ads, cut it and move to the next product instead of pouring more money in. [L0950.001]
- Kill a product early when it shows no signs. Test about $50 to $100 on day one, with a maximum of $200 to $250. On TikTok, consider killing it around $60 if creatives and CTR are poor. [L1436.084]
- Give a product three to five days after fixing the offer and the store. If about $200 has been spent across 10 to 15 audiences at $5 to $10 per day without any sign of profitability, move on to the next product. [L1345.079]
- Spend is one of the limits, but the numbers decide. Before running ads, a dropshipping seller should know cost of goods sold, break-even return on ad spend, and break-even cost per purchase. These numbers define the limits within which every later scaling decision is made. [L0875.001]
- Switch off an ad that spends past its margin without a sale. In the example below, the margin is too thin to scale at the current CPA, so raise average order value as well. [L0995.019]

**Kill thresholds from different stages (sources do not conflict; each applies at a different stage):**

| Stage | Threshold | When it applies | Units |
|---|---|---|---|
| Day one, no signs | $50 to $100; maximum $200 to $250 | Facebook test, no signs on day one | [L1436.084] |
| TikTok, poor creatives and CTR | About $60 | TikTok test with weak creatives and CTR | [L1436.084] |
| Offer and store fixed | About $200 across 10 to 15 audiences at $5 to $10 per day, after 3 to 5 days | No sign of profitability | [L1345.079] |
| Zero sales after spend | About $300 | Product with no sales; treat spend as learning money | [L1432.023] |
| Per-product test budget | About $200 to $250 to $300 | One product per week until there is enough data | [L1176.039] |
| Share of capital | Maximum 50 percent of investment capital | Per new product test | [L1251.035] |

- Do not drop a product too early when its videos show some views, because the breakthrough can come unexpectedly after a stretch of low results. In the referenced case the seller was close to quitting just before the product exploded. [L0821.011]
- Judge a product by its early signals. If it gets views but no sales after the normal test window, extend the test by one more week. If it gets neither views nor engagement, and you doubt it converts, drop it and move to the next product. [L0820.002]
- Do not close ad sets too early. Some ad sets only convert after the spend passes a certain amount (for example after the 12th dollar), so give ad sets patience while CTR is good. [L0642.048]
- Be patient with higher-ticket products (for example a $59.99 price). The pixel needs time to collect data, so give the ads more spend before closing them, as long as CTR is above 1.5%. [L0642.047]
- The 2% CTR case: a student started with a product costing $26.99 that sold for $49.99, leaving a $23 margin. On the first day of Facebook ads they got a 2% click-through rate and no sales. On day two they ran six ad sets with $10 each, with a $20 offer running. Advice given: switch off an ad that spends past its margin without a sale, and raise average order value with a bundle, since a margin of about $23 makes scaling hard when CPA is $15 to $20. [L0995.019]
- Quality of testing matters more than volume in the early phase. A product tested with only a few attempts gives little information. Set a clear test-count target before deciding a product is not a winner, for example 20 to 30 products rather than three. [L0691.028]
- Judging a new product after only one week and one sale is too early, especially if several offers have not been tested yet. [L1131.044]
- Early signals and dropping: a product that shows weak early signals should not be kept out of hope. Early signals decide the next step. [L0820.002]

### Early signals and when to judge

- Procedure for a new product that performs weakly:
  1. Run a new product for the first couple of days.
  2. Check its performance against your KPIs.
  3. If performance is weak, send your data to coaches or mentors for evaluation.
  4. Wait for their assessment and feedback.
  5. Follow their recommendation to either cut the product or continue testing.
  [L0037.014]
- Poor early results were held on to for too long in one case. The team saw weak performance within two to three days and changed the background and angle on day three, which is an early enough point to pivot. [L0764.020]
- Daily results swing between green and red days, so do not cut a campaign after one red day. Add more creatives and let the system keep learning. [L1019.047]
- A slow week after sales in past weeks can be normal. Check the funnel (queries, add-to-cart, checkout intent) and follow the troubleshooting steps before making changes. [L0666.046]
- Low ad spend produces slower data, so a small budget will not quickly show whether a product works. Judge test results only after enough spend has produced enough data. [L1038.008]
- Early traffic behaviour is random at low volume. A new store may make two or three sales in the first days and then go weeks without a sale. Let data accumulate over about three weeks before major changes. [L0856.110]
- A low number of sales can come from a sample that is too small to read. The first step is to grow traffic before judging a store as failed. [L0855.172]
- When few sales come in, do not change niches right away. First add more approved suppliers and optimise ads on the products that already get quality clicks. [L0835.131]
- The first sales depend on traffic rather than on a fixed amount of time. Once a store receives around 100 unique visitors from Google Shopping, the conversion target can be checked. Note: this advice is from 2020 and is older. [L1111.171]
- Winning products are found through fast, repeated product testing, so a beginner may need several failed products (for example around eight) before finding a winner. [L1394.002]
- Judge a hook only after repeated uploads of the same format with variations, usually three to six times. Videos that look weak early can still produce a breakout, and one successful video can lift the whole account's follower count. [L1383.026]
- Influencer results are not always visible immediately. Be patient while the sales funnel works, and use the full funnel to qualify influencers over a longer period rather than judging a single post. [L0790.087]
- A product that works at first and then stops being profitable when scaled is normal. Drop it, launch the next product, and record what was learned from each product so the next launch is better. [L1107.004]
- A single viral result does not prove a product will last. Keep testing new products and angles to build a consistent pipeline. [L0706.058]
- Winning on TikTok is not a single lucky product. Nobody in the referenced group found one product and executed it properly on the first try. The probability of a hit rises with the number of products tested. [L0821.020]
- Dropshipping results come from testing, not from research alone. Do not give up on a product too early, but keep checking the data. [L1108.026]
- Ad spend on a product with zero sales is learning money, as noted above. Use the first sales only as proof of the process, not as the goal. [L1440.053]
- A case with strong early revenue: one operator reached roughly $1,000 in daily revenue on the second day after launching a new product. That result came only after testing more than twelve products before finding the first winner. [L1454.019]
- Pinterest case: a dropshipping product in another account generated over $2,000 in its first week on Pinterest, with a ROAS of about 4.5 shortly after launch. Testing was done with a small daily budget and the account was still in the learning stage. [L1135.047]

### One product at a time

- Beginners should test one product at a time, with one account per product. Running several products in parallel splits attention and makes the learning curve harder to manage. [L0820.004]
- Test one product at a time with full focus. Move on to a new product only after the current one fails to get traction within about one to two weeks. Once a product converts, keep it and open more accounts to push it rather than switching products. [L1186.032]
- When working alone, test one product at a time for about two weeks, with two products at most. Spreading small effort across many products usually fails. Commit fully to one product, then expand once you are more experienced. [L1444.012]
- Testing one product at a time is the discipline; testing a single product, deciding it is a scam or saturated, and quitting is the most common failure pattern. [L1038.002]
- Testing multiple different products in rapid succession, rather than focusing on one product through all six phases, disrupts the learning cycle and prevents reaching profitability by Phase 6. Consistency requires staying with one product long enough to gather meaningful data. [L0199.019]
- Testing sequence for one winning candidate:
  1. Test one product at a time.
  2. Try different store designs.
  3. Try different prices.
  4. Try different bundles.
  5. Try different audiences.
  6. Once a winner is found, scale it.
  7. If the results are not what you need, move on to the next product.
  [L1176.040]
- Patience with one product: a store takes months to form its brand, so keep a product in testing until its data confirms it works before adding more products to the store. [L0871.086]
- A failed brand can be tested again once the operator has improved offers, upsells and creatives. Apply the lesson from the first attempt before retrying, and do not use it as a reason to abandon the brand category. [L1041.036]

### Test pace and volume

- Set a testing schedule of about one or two products per week. Expect to test several products before finding a first winner. Focus on controlling the inputs of testing, because results follow from consistent testing. [L0347.033]
- Test one product per week with a budget of about 200 to 300 USD per product until there is enough data to move on or keep trying. [L1176.039]
- Patience decides how many products you can test. Choose a testing pace you can sustain. Testing several products with high effort and all failing kills motivation. [L1171.048]
- Some operators can test up to 15 products when they have the energy, which raises the chance that one of them becomes a winner. [L1171.049]
- Spam testing only fits sellers who already know a very specific niche and have a large budget to test many random products. Without that knowledge and budget it depends on luck and fails, since it needs funds for 50 to 60 products. [L1158.046]
- Winning in dropshipping depends on continuous testing of many products and ads over time. Each attempt has a low hit rate, and more attempts raise the chance of finding a winner. Stopping after a few failures makes a winner very unlikely. [L1038.001]
- Expect to test many products before finding a winner. In the example, the first winner took about three months and about six tested products. [L1131.043]
- Test a product or strategy long enough before judging it. A common budget is about 30 products before reassessing whether the approach works. Most people quit after one or two tries, which is usually too early. [L1128.022]
- Do not wait for the one perfect winner before testing. Keep testing many products until you see real progress, because a winner may not arrive on its own. [L1444.013]
- Taking action and testing many products matters more than finding the perfect first product. Not every product will be a winner, so keep testing rather than endlessly searching. [L0561.052]
- Find winners through volume and speed of testing, not through perfect products or perfect ads. [L0887.016]

**Number of products before a first winner (sources differ; each reflects a different context):**

| Source | Number of products | Context | Units |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mentor's experience | Around the 8th product, range 5th to 20th | Beginners | [L1107.003] |
| Experienced sellers | About 5 products; TikTok process about 7 to 8 product tests | Experienced sellers and TikTok | [L1382.066] |
| Worked example | About 6 products over about 3 months | Single example | [L1131.043] |
| Minimum before judging | At least 12 products | Before judging whether the approach works | [L0444.015] |
| Test-count target | 20 to 30 products | Before deciding a product is not a winner | [L0691.028] |
| Reassessment budget | About 30 products | Before reassessing the approach | [L1128.022] |
| Operator example | 65 products over about 8 months | One winner, scaled to roughly 200k USD revenue | [L1038.023] |
| Spam testing | 50 to 60 products | Only with specific niche knowledge and large budget | [L1158.046] |
| Before first winner (Source D) | More than 12 products | Case with $1,000 daily revenue on day two | [L1454.019] |

- Plan for many failed products before a winner appears. Expecting the first winner within the first three or four products is unrealistic, although it is not impossible. [L1454.020]
- Plan for the first product, and often the second product, to fail, since neither is likely to be a winner. Expect several test rounds before finding a product that sells profitably. [L1394.019]
- Most beginners do not find a first winning product on the first try. Keep testing products consistently and stay patient. [L1107.003]
- Taking a first product all the way to the scaling stage is already a strong result for a beginner, since many people never get that far. A second product can still fail, so one failed launch does not mean the method is wrong. Apply what the earlier product taught you to the next launch. [L1107.038]
- Finish studying the complete programme and test at least 12 products before judging whether the approach works. Testing 12 products is the point where beginners typically start to see results, and most beginners who fail quit before reaching it. [L0444.015]
- Judging a new product after only one week and one sale is too early (see the kill rules section). [L1131.044]
- A beginner with no website or online advertising experience can learn both from freely available online guidance and then improve through repeated tests. Expect early ad spend without returns during this phase, and track results from the first campaign so that losses stay within a set limit. Note: this advice is from 2021 to 2022 and is older. [L1014.022]

### Patience with data

- Be patient when a product is higher ticket, and give ads more spend before closing them while CTR is above 1.5%. [L0642.047]
- The start is the hardest phase because every test costs money before a winner is found. Once a product is making money, spending on testing becomes much easier. [L0692.046]
- Losing money on ads is a common reason people quit e-commerce, while organic work mainly costs time rather than cash. Starting with organic reduces the risk of spending a large budget without results. [L0819.018]
- Viewing a product test as time spent rather than money lost is a valid way to keep testing without pressure. Frustration after each failed product is common, and forcing results quickly tends to make testing harder. [L0819.025]
- Judge a product using the test window and the data, not the first few clicks. [L0562.036]
- Influencer results are not visible immediately; qualify influencers over a longer period using the full funnel. [L0790.087]
- Be patient while a product's data accumulates: the early-traffic randomness described above means major changes should wait about three weeks. [L0856.110]
- In a slow period, take a short break to rest and plan ahead, then use the time to tighten spending on low-converting keywords instead of cutting all paid traffic. Tightening is more effective than a full stop. Note: this advice is from 2021 to 2022 and is older. [L0949.061]
- A product's kill rule may need more data before it can be judged, but a fixed test spend still decides. [L0950.001]
- Build a sustainable pace with patience: "Patience decides how many products you can test." [L1171.048]
- Be patient with the number of products tested; do not expect results from one trial. [L1444.013]
- Keep spare capital so that a slow product does not force an early decision. [L1251.036]
- Ad-level patience: Facebook ad sets may need more spend before they show whether they convert. [L0642.048]
- Hook patience: judge a hook after three to six repeated uploads. [L1383.026]

### Data over emotion

- Emotional attachment to products can cloud optimisation decisions. Do not decide to bid higher or retain a product based on hope that it will improve. Base all pausing and bidding decisions on statistical thresholds and historical data, not on optimism about future performance. [L0299.027]
- Do not become emotionally attached to a product; decide to kill or keep it based on test data. Failed stores and products should be cut quickly to free budget for new tests. [L0660.043]
- Treat ad testing as a data-driven game. Decide based on CTR, store conversion rate and profit, rather than falling in love with a product after investing time and money into it. [L0650.052]
- Do not become emotionally tied to a product you have invested heavily in. The effort put in does not guarantee output, so judge it by the numbers. [L0950.002]
- Do not let emotional attachment drive decisions. A product that looks good to you may fail the numbers, and a product that looks weak may sell. [L1161.046]
- Use the numbers on the screen as the only signal of whether something works, including for changes to ads, pages and budgets. [L1038.005]
- Compare each product test with earlier data, rather than with how the idea feels. [L1038.019]

### Letting go of products

- Pick products you actually enjoy testing. Products you dislike are often dropped after four days to a week and a half of daily uploads. Lack of motivation kills tests before they produce data. [L0706.009]
- Set a kill rule before you test and follow it. Do not get attached to one dropshipping product, since a failing product blocks budget that another product could use. [L0648.041]
- After a failed product, the fastest comeback is to reuse a proven creative and page template and switch to a product that is already selling in the market. Do not build fresh creatives from scratch. [L0950.003]
- When a product works at first and then stops being profitable at scale, drop it and launch the next product. [L1107.004]
- Keep the next product in the testing queue. A product that flops should be replaced by the next product in the queue, not treated as proof that the model does not work. [L1038.002]
- A product that has a strong visual wow factor but no clear problem it solves often gets views without sales. Keep the kill rule even when the product looks impressive. [L0501.039]
- Lean on the kill rule when the product is one you like: "Set kill rules for a product before you test it and follow them, because keeping a product you like without results wastes time." [L0501.039]
- Do not stay with an idea just because it sounded good; test it and let the result decide. [L1315.057]

### Expecting failure

- Expect many ideas to fail. Some tests will not work, and a few may cause problems such as banned ad accounts. Treat failed tests as part of the process and keep testing. [L0334.055]
- Enjoy the process of testing, because brand, entry point and offer tests take time and many of them will fail before one works. [L0334.056]
- Expect many conversion tests to fail and treat each failure as a learning step. Plan tests so that you know what is being tested and what the results are, then change the approach for the next test. Note: this advice is from 2021 to 2022 and is older. [L0400.014]
- Most products fail in testing, and no formula reliably finds a winner on the first try. Test many products, learn from each failure, and improve the process rather than expecting a shortcut. [L1436.083]
- Expect many test products to fail. Repeated iteration is part of the process, and a failed launch is not a verdict on the whole business. [L0950.004]
- There is no shortcut formula that guarantees a winning product on the first try. Winners come from testing many products, learning from losses, improving the process and the branding, and accepting that a few products will fail. [L1382.065]
- The first and often the second product are likely to fail, so plan for several test rounds. [L1394.019]
- Losses are a normal part of testing. Before seeing any profit, a beginner should expect to lose about $2,000 or the full budget, as noted above. [L1394.003]
- Testing a product is a risk: a product can fail quickly, so keep spare capital. [L1251.036]
- Intuition often misjudges the market, so expect to be wrong often. [L1261.134]
- Plan for failure as the default and treat each failed product as information. [L1436.083]
- Failure is feedback, not the end: "A failed test is feedback, not failure. Test, refine and improve until the product or approach wins." [L0631.012]
- Many ideas fail; keep testing, measure the results and adjust quickly. [L1153.078]
- Ad-level failure is common: "Some tests will not work, and a few may cause problems such as banned ad accounts." [L0334.055]
- Influencer campaigns often fail, and some of the first creators contacted will not convert. [L1379.047]
- The start is the hardest phase, because every test costs money before a winner is found. [L0692.046]
- Failure in one area does not mean the model fails. Apply what each failure teaches to the next product. [L1107.038]

### Systems for steady output

- The formula for sustained success in dropshipping is the consistent repetition of three elements: a good product, strong creatives and a good funnel, executed repeatedly over time. Without this cycle, sustainable profitability cannot be achieved. [L0199.016]
- Build a consistent pipeline of products and angles. A single result does not prove a product will last. [L0706.058]
- Create a routine: test, read metrics, improve, and record. Over time the routine builds the skills to recognise winners faster. [L1038.019]
- Record what was learned from each product so the next launch is better. [L1107.004]
- Write down the expected testing loss before starting, and keep the testing budget separate from the core business. [L1394.003]
- Treat each product as a single cycle in a repeatable system. Judge it by its data, record the result and move to the next product if the data says so. [L1440.053]
- Winning in dropshipping comes from volume and speed of testing; a stalled result shows what does not work and brings you closer to what does. [L0887.016]
- Use a fixed testing budget and fixed kill rules as the system, so that each product gets the same treatment. [L0335.024]
- Test many products in a steady rhythm and do not wait for the one perfect winner. [L1444.013]
- Store and brand building happen after the product is proven, not before. [L0871.086]
- Constant testing creates a stream of creatives and products to choose from. [L0352.053]
- Use a cut rule before the test starts and follow it strictly. [L0648.041]
- The cycle of testing, measuring and adjusting repeats for each product. [L1038.019]
- Keep testing new products and angles to build a consistent pipeline. [L0706.058]

### Scaling, automation and channel notes

- Procedure for paid traffic automation work:
  1. Test automated bidding with a controlled experiment.
  2. Improve the product feed to get more traffic.
  3. Use Smart Shopping in controlled tests, not on the whole account at once.
  4. Shift the time saved to higher-leverage activities.
  [L0454.010]
- Automating everything at once and checking performance only about once a month lets underperformance run for weeks unnoticed. Introduce automation in steps with regular review. Blind trust in automated results is the most common error. [L0454.011]
- Automation works like an exoskeleton: it lets the marketer handle much more volume without more strain, but it does not replace judgement. Use it to get more done with the same effort. [L0454.041]
- The basics of paid advertising stay the same whatever the interface: show the right message to the right person at the right time. If a campaign ignores this, for example by targeting an audience that has no reason to buy the product, it will underperform regardless of tool changes. [L0489.004]
- Scaling speed differed among operators. One reached 25k plus per month within two months from start. Another needed six months before mentorship and three months after. One scaled within three days once a winning product and offer were found. The bottleneck was finding a working offer, not the scaling itself. [L1469.021]
- Build the mindset of right traffic, right time and right price before setting up any campaigns, because the later campaign work depends on these concepts. Checking targeting against the funnel stage and the cost of acquisition is the core test for every paid campaign. Paid ad management should be based on profitability rather than raw traffic volume. Note: this advice is from 2021 to 2022 and is older. [L1507.013]
- Ad testing decisions are made on CTR, store conversion rate and profit. [L0650.052]
- Ad-account quality: the bigger the account, the more the testing loss can grow. [L1394.005]
- A winning product found in one test may need an offer change before it scales. [L1469.021]
- Test before scaling, and do not scale a product that has not shown data. [L1335.031]
- A single operator scaled one winner to roughly 200k USD in revenue after 65 products. Afterwards the brand, run as a custom brand, was still growing day over day and month over month. [L1038.023]

#### Further details

- Test new and unconventional ideas if the risk is low and potential loss is small; assess the downside before rejecting ideas, then measure their impact on the brand. [L0260.024]

- A fall sock product was a one-size-fits-all product with three pack sizes and a broad audience of moms, teens and Q4 gift buyers. The store was put up in about 15 minutes, and the lifestyle and emotional ads were mixed and matched to fit the fall aesthetic. The seller's conversion rate was strong, and the product was scaled for a period before the season ended, with a note that it would not sell in summer and would fade as a trend. [L0347.037]

## Handle setbacks, emotions and the long game

### Endurance and the long game

- Entrepreneurship is an endurance effort with emotional swings, not a sprint. Expect long periods of doubt and plan for them, rather than assuming steady effort guarantees steady results. [L0449.012]
- Running a business is a long endurance effort with emotional highs and lows. Your mind can invent stories about failure, so base your judgment on actual figures rather than feelings. [L0599.009]
- Paid traffic is a long-running effort, not a quick win. Judge progress by year-over-year and quarter-over-quarter growth, stay consistent with the plan, and keep improving it over time. [L1203.037]
- Linear, predictable growth does not happen in e-commerce. Expect large peaks and large lows as a normal part of the business. [L1042.004]
- Burnout comes from repeated failures without rest. Expecting failure in advance keeps your effort sustainable over the long term, and long-term persistence in e-commerce depends on this. [L1394.014]
- Succeeding as an entrepreneur requires a higher tolerance for short-term pain, such as hard work and uncomfortable tasks. If you are not getting the results you want despite working hard, you are probably avoiding a specific kind of pain. [L0701.011]
- Treat building an online business as a marathon meant to run for years, not a sprint. Set realistic expectations about pace and do not expect everything to be finished within a week. [L1496.004]
- High-ticket dropshipping has no hidden trick that separates winners from losers. Results depend mainly on the operator's focus, consistency and patience, not on knowing a secret strategy. [L0419.001]
- Three qualities are required to succeed with an e-commerce business model: focus, consistency and patience. Build these deliberately, because they determine whether you reach the goal. [L0419.003]
- Most operators fail at the consistency stage, not at the knowledge stage. They do not apply the marketing work repeatedly over months. A system produces predictable growth only when its parts are run every week without long gaps. [L0414.052]
- Consistency and commitment decide success more than any single method. Owners who quit or switch to a new idea each time something feels hard rarely build a profitable store. [L0771.056]
- Change does not happen overnight. Real results often come only after many long working hours, many tested products and repeated mistakes. [L0631.014]
- Consistency combined with trust in the process is what lets a beginner later recognize a turning point. Keep executing the same routine steadily instead of expecting a single breakthrough moment. [L0631.016]
